Curvature is unknown where r″ is ill-conditioned (the stencil and its half step disagree, as for the evolute), flat (κ = 0) where the acceleration across the tangent `(r′ × r″)/|r′|` is within ten times that disagreement or below the evolute's own guard, so a straight stretch's rounding reads as flat, and its center is at infinity, not drawn, beyond 100 radii of the study (the robust radius of the base). Each is counted. A sample has no tangent where its speed is below 10⁻⁹ of its distance from the origin plus the study radius, per unit of t.

**Drawing and motion.** The drawing shows the osculating circle and its center, T and N, each 70 page units long, and the point's construction lines (the evolute's normal to the center, the involute's string, an offset's normal, a caustic's ray, an inversion's correspondence, a rolling arm, a pedal-type curve's foot with the perpendicular from the pole) in the probe's inks, which the 3D notebook shares; a stack or circle family has no single point to highlight. Unless it stands between samples (below), the probe snaps to samples, so moving it never recomputes. **Move the probe along the curve** moves it from the first sample at the start to the last at the end over the fixed study. While parameters vary, every frame is computed with diagnostics and the probe stays at its t, keeps its share of the drawn length, or moves along the curve, exactly as in the [3D notebook](#spatial-curvature-torsion-and-the-parameter-probe), with the same rule for each (`probe.ts`). Animations that keep the study fixed draw the probe at the user's sample, as **The probe in fixed animations** under [the spatial probe](#spatial-curvature-torsion-and-the-parameter-probe) states for both notebooks. While light is traced, the mirror is drawn throughout, and so is its probe, but a caustic's ray is highlighted only once the light reaches the caustic point at its end. On **Sunlight gathering in an oval mirror**, parallel light reflected at a point whose radius of curvature is ρ, at incidence θ, gathers on the caustic ρ·cos θ/2 along the reflected ray, while the ray's chord of the osculating circle is 2ρ·cos θ long: the probe's ray ends a quarter of the way along that chord, so 2|Q − P|² = (Q − P)·(C − P) for the point P, the caustic point Q and the center of curvature C. A browser test checks this identity in page coordinates.

**Between samples.** With **At any t, between samples** (`probe.between`), the probe stands at the parameter `t = min·(1 − u) + max·u` for its place u in the domain, and Go describes the base there (`engine/probe.go`, asked by `probe`, a parameter or a share of the length): its point, frame, curvature and center by the same function the samples use, with their guards, so at a sample it reads exactly what the sample does; the arc length at the last sample at or before t, continued over the rest of the way by Simpson's rule on the speed at its ends and midpoint; and the construction's input and point from the evaluators refinement uses (see [Refinement between samples](#refinement-between-samples)), so the highlighted construction is the construction's own at t. The base is undefined at t wherever a sample there would be: where it is not finite, or its derivatives the construction needs are ill-conditioned. A share s of the length places it where the drawn length is s times the whole: at a sample whose length is exactly that, and otherwise by bisection within the drawn interval that passes it. A chase or trajectory, integrated step by step, has no evaluator at an arbitrary t and keeps snapping to its samples. Over a drawn study the notebook asks Go for the probe alone, which leaves the study's result untouched; the drawing is busy until it arrives. Go keeps the last study it computed for the probe (without refinement, which the probe never reads), so a move over the same study evaluates one point, and the notebook keeps one such request in flight, asking for the latest place when it returns, so a drag never queues work. While the parameters vary, Go places it with each frame: at its t (absent where the frame's domain leaves it out), at the same share of the frame's drawn length, or at the frame's own share of the domain. Drawing along the curve reaches it once the sample after it is drawn; traced light reaches its construction when the light's arrival, interpolated linearly between the samples on either side, has passed.

**Checks.** Native tests verify the ellipse's curvature `ab/(a² sin²t + b² cos²t)^{3/2}`, tangent, left normal and evolute center; a clockwise circle's κ = −1/R about its center; invariance under a rigid motion and a regular reparameterization; a line flat at every sample; the cubic's inflection flat with centers at infinity beside it; arc length exact on a circle and fourth order on (t, sin 3t) against an independent quadrature; and nothing counted across a gap or a left-out sample, with an ill-conditioned r″ unknown. Between samples, they verify that the probe at every tested sample equals that sample's diagnostics and points bit for bit (its construction point within rounding of the sample loop's); the ellipse's curvature, frame and center and its evolute's point at parameters off the samples; arc length exact on a circle and, on an ellipse, no farther from an independent Gauss–Legendre length than the samples are; the involute and pedal of a circle and an evolute's pedal against closed forms; a share of the length on a circle at that share of the domain and on a wave at that share of its length; nothing in a gap, no point where the evolute's r″ is ill-conditioned, and no center beside the cubic's inflection; that asking for the probe leaves the rest of the result byte-identical; that the probe alone, from the kept study, is a fresh refined computation's, through changes of study and back; and the refusals of a malformed query and of integrated and unparametrized curves. The WASM bridge checks the probe alone against the full result's. The browser tests check the readout and drawing against the closed forms in page coordinates, at a sample and between samples, in playback, scrubbing and export.

## Line weights

**Line weight** in the 2D and 4D notebooks offers the 3D notebook's [weights](#spatial-line-weights) (`web/line-weight.ts`): each SVG stroke keeps its own width at **Regular**, the drawing as it always was, and is scaled by 0.6 at **Fine** and 1.6 at **Bold**, rounded to four decimals; a **Hairline** is one pixel at any size (`vector-effect: non-scaling-stroke`), so it grows fainter as an export grows larger. Marks drawn without a stroke stay unstroked. The live drawing, stills, SVG files, videos and the loop check draw the chosen weight; exports record it when it is not regular.

## Still exports: size and transparent background

**Export image** draws each still at a whole multiple k ∈ {1, 2, 3, 4} of the drawing's own page: 1000 × 760, or a 4D pair's 2000 × 760 or 1000 × 1520. The default k = 2 is the size every still had before it could be chosen. In 2D and 4D the size sets the PNG only; their SVG is a vector and keeps its own size. In 3D it sets every format: the PNG, the SVG around it, and the **Lines (SVG)** page. The drawing is rendered at its target size, never enlarged from a smaller raster. Its camera, layers and theme are those of the live drawing. Strokes keep their share of the page ([line weights](#line-weights)), and a hairline stays one pixel, so it is fainter in a larger file.

**Transparent background** leaves the page's background out. In 2D and 4D the background is a rectangle filling an svg's viewBox: the page, or each panel of a 4D pair. The export leaves those rectangles and the root's CSS background out of a copy of the drawing, rather than painting over them. Rasterizing that copy onto a canvas with alpha gives each antialiased edge its coverage as alpha. A seed marker filled with the paper color keeps its fill, since it is part of the drawing.

The 3D drawing is composited by WebGL, and every way it puts color on its page is linear in the background color B. Opaque fragments replace it. A blended stroke gives `a·c + (1 − a)·B`, and multisampled coverage averages samples. See-through sheets give `B·(1 − α)ⁿ + c̄·(1 − (1 − α)ⁿ)` ([see-through sheets](#spatial-see-through-sheets-and-hidden-lines)). Each pixel is therefore `P + T·B`, where P is everything drawn, premultiplied, and T is what lets the background through. The transparent export draws the page twice with the same program as the opaque drawing, over black (B = 0) and over white (B = 1). Over black the page is P; over white it is P + T. Its alpha is `1 − T`, where T is the mean of the three channels' differences between the two pages, and its straight color is `P/(1 − T)`. For see-through sheets this restates the rule with alpha: alpha `1 − (1 − α)ⁿ` and premultiplied color `c̄·(1 − (1 − α)ⁿ)`. Where the drawing covers its page, T = 0, and the transparent file has exactly the opaque drawing's ink. A transparent still costs two renders.

Files record a setting only when it is not the default, so default files are unchanged. A 3D SVG's metadata records `image` (its width and height) and `background: "transparent"`. A 2D or 4D SVG's metadata records the background.

**Device limits.** A page the device cannot draw is refused with its size named, rather than saved blank or smaller. A 2D canvas can come back with no context, or with one that draws nothing, as iOS Safari does past its canvas area. One pixel drawn and read back in the far corner tells which. WebGL refuses a page larger than its renderbuffer or viewport limits, or one whose drawing buffer was allocated smaller than asked. See-through sheets also need a texture of the page's size, and twice its side for samples (see [see-through sheets](#spatial-see-through-sheets-and-hidden-lines)); a page that fits only the first is drawn with one sample a pixel, not refused. The visible-only line drawing's work limit grows in proportion to the page's area (200 million at 2000 × 1520), so a view that exports at one size exports at every size. The limits are asked before a size is offered: the menu lists still sizes from the smallest up to the first this device cannot draw, and a chosen size that does not fit is kept while the largest that does is used. A 2D canvas is tried at each size and released. 3D adds the WebGL limits above, the texture limit when sheets are seen through or hidden lines fade behind each layer, and the canvas for a transparent still's matte. Animation exports stop the resolution slider the same way. The refusals remain for what cannot be asked in advance: a drawing buffer allocated smaller, or a limit that shrinks after a size last drew.

**Checks.** The browser tests decode each PNG in Node, independently of the browser that wrote it (`tests/png.ts`). They check the chosen dimensions in every notebook, including a 4D pair, and an alpha channel that is clear at the page's corner. Laid over the theme's background, the transparent file reproduces the opaque one at the same size to within one level where its ink is opaque, and to within a third of a level on average across the page. That holds for 2D, a 4D projection and a pair, and in 3D for sampled strokes, bold strokes dashed behind a tube, and see-through sheets. A 4× PNG matches the exported SVG rasterized directly at that size (mean difference 0 in Chromium), and the default PNG enlarged to that size differs from the SVG by 0.1 to 0.5 levels. Unit tests check the matte against analytic coverage, including see-through layers `1 − (1 − α)ⁿ`, over three backgrounds. Default exports were confirmed byte-identical to those made before this setting, for all three notebooks, both themes, and see-through and stroked 3D presets.

**Live and exported drawings.** `tests/export-equivalence.spec.ts` checks that a still is the drawing shown, after the camera has been moved, with the page pinned to 1000 × 760 CSS pixels (2000 × 760 for a 4D pair) at a device pixel ratio of 1. In 3D the 1 × PNG is the live canvas pixel for pixel (equal hashes of the decoded pixels) for explicitly selected hairlines and regular strokes over a ribbon, bold strokes dashed behind a tube, and see-through sheets (sampled two by two), in both themes. Each case checks the weight actually drawn. With the sheet hidden, every line of **Lines (SVG) · every line** lies within a pixel of the canvas's lines and the reverse, in the same inks, and the file records the shown camera. In 2D, a 4D projection and a 4D pair, the saved SVG's path, transform and polygon coordinates match the live element's, and its rasterization at 1 × matches the saved PNG with a mean difference under half a level per channel, checking viewport geometry, primitive coordinates and inks too. The 1 × PNG matches a screenshot of the drawing as the page paints it: each one's ink lies within a pixel of the other's, and they differ by under 2.5 levels a channel per inked pixel, against about 50 for a one-pixel shift. The visible-only line drawing is not compared with the canvas, whose shaded sheets are ink too.

## Numerical policy

Derivatives use five-point Lagrange stencils at spacing `domain_span × 10⁻⁴`, shifted to remain inside the domain at endpoints; a derived input's base derivatives use three times that spacing. First and second derivatives of the curve a construction acts on are compared against a half-step stencil to reject ill-conditioned evaluations. Direction derivatives use the same bounded stencil. The spacing follows the domain, not `samples`, so a term oscillating much faster than about 250 times per 2π of domain leaves r″ ill-conditioned at many samples whatever the sample count: at 625, about a fifth of the samples' curvature was unknown, and samples near the check's threshold passed or failed differently once translated. A preset keeps its highest frequency below that. Curvature and ray-envelope denominators have explicit tolerances.

The engine returns `null` at omitted points instead of serializing NaN or infinity. The renderer does not connect across nulls and additionally breaks very long screen-space segments. These are heuristics; arbitrary expressions and undersampled oscillations cannot be guaranteed continuous. The UI reports omitted samples and exposes resolution. Native tests cover analytic curves, endpoint stencils, integration convergence, optical invariants, and pathological inputs. [Refinement between samples](#refinement-between-samples) adds points where chords stray, as an opt-in heuristic; certified branch separation remains future work before claiming higher precision. [Scale and translation](#scale-and-translation) states the error budgets the 2D engine meets when a study is scaled or moved.

Reveal animations expose progressively larger prefixes of the final numerical grid, retaining the original `t_min` arc-length anchor. Their temporal resolution and spatial resolution are independent. Parameter animations instead construct a fresh curve/ray family at each displayed parameter value. Numerical singularities can appear or disappear as parameters move; output curves are never blended across such transitions. Increasing duration increases temporal granularity at a given achieved frame rate, not the number of spatial samples.

Point sources may be specified as Cartesian x/y or polar radius and theta. Radius is nonnegative, theta is in radians counterclockwise from +x, and Go resolves the source as `(r cos(theta), r sin(theta))`. A polar angle animation interpolates the angle itself without wrapping, then resolves the Cartesian position for each frame; it follows an arc rather than a chord. The result includes the effective Cartesian source position for consistent rendering. Coordinate controls in the frontend convert representations when switching editors; the numerical engine remains authoritative for each computation.

## Scale and translation

A study and its image under a similarity `p ↦ s·p + c` should draw the same thing: the image of every point, with the same gaps, breaks, warnings and diagnostics. `engine/scale_test.go` checks this for every 2D preset, read from `engine/testdata/presets.json` (a copy of `web/presets.ts`, which the WASM bridge test keeps current; the test file says how to regenerate it), with refinement and diagnostics wherever the format has them. A copy scales every length by s (radii, arms, distances, offsets, the stack, rolling shapes and tracing points, speeds, the capture distance, the escape radius) and maps every position (pole, source, inversion center, pursuers, seeds, a level set's window). Expressions are wrapped as `s·(x) + cₓ`; a level set's function reads `((x − cₓ)/s, (y − c_y)/s)`; a translated polar, roulette, Lissajous or Fourier curve, and a polar source, are rewritten as Cartesian ones. The parameter, angles, indices, counts and `a` are unchanged. Two definitions cannot carry the map: an iterated map's formulas are fixed, and a vector field's escape circle is centered on the origin, so a field is only scaled.

**Scaled by a power of two**, s = 2⁻²⁰ (about 10⁻⁶) and 2¹³ (about 8000): binary floating point scales exactly, so the copy must be the exact image. Every coordinate, refined point, curvature and center is the original's times s, bit for bit, and every gap, break, count, warning, refinement and probe classification is the same. Any difference is a comparison with an absolute constant.

**Translated** by c, about 860 and 36,000 units with s = 1, and 860 × 2⁻²⁰ with s = 2⁻²⁰: each coordinate is rounded at the larger magnitude, and a stencil derivative divides that rounding by its spacing h = 10⁻⁴ of the domain, once per derivative. With L the copy's diameter (twice the study radius), ρ = 1 + |c|/L, and u = 2⁻⁵³, a point of the copy lies within

  `256·u·ρ·10^(4k)·(L + its distance from the study's median point)`

of the original's image. Here k counts the stencil derivatives the path reads: 0 for the base and a chord's far end, 1 for pedal-type curves, offsets, involutes, rolling traces and line or circle envelopes, and 2 for evolutes, caustics and the probe's centers. A derived input adds its own k. The constant 256 covers the one-sided stencils at the ends of the domain, whose coefficients carry about seven times the centered stencil's rounding; the worst observed ratio is about 110. A chase or trajectory is integrated to relative local errors of 10⁻¹² and 10⁻¹⁰, and its paths are held to 10⁻⁸. Curvature κ is compared as κL/(1 + |κ|L), which measures it as a curvature on near-straight stretches and as a radius where the curve turns sharply, each where it is well conditioned. Refinement and a level set's crossings may place their points differently, so each point must lie within the same budget, plus twice refinement's tolerance (2 × 10⁻⁴ of a radius) or two thousandths of a cell, of the original's image polyline. Gaps, breaks, warnings and counts must match, except that an inflection may be called flat or have its center beyond 100 radii: neither has a center.

**Limit.** A construction on an evolute (an involute or offset of an evolute) reads three derivatives, past this budget. It keeps its gaps only to about ρ ≈ 5. A few hundred diameters out, the half-step check on its input rejects every sample. Its translated copies are not checked. Nor is a copy whose budget for centers, at k = 2, exceeds its own size: its curvature is rounding there, as for **Swallowtails at a star's point**, a few thousandths of a unit across, carried 36,000 units out.

**Tolerances made relative.** Each of these was found by a copy that failed, and each failure was reproduced before the fix:

- `refine.Radius` floors its radius at 10⁻⁴ and an axis's spread at 10⁻¹². At 10⁻⁶ scale these set refinement's tolerance and the probe's 100-radius clip. The 2D engine now measures points in the power of two at or below their interquartile spread (`studyRadius`). That is exact, so the radius is unchanged wherever the floors did not apply. A spread within 2⁻⁴⁰ of the quartiles' magnitude is rounding, as on the branch of **Circles through a focus** that is the focus alone, and such points are measured in that magnitude instead: their rounding is not taken for a shape to refine.
- The half-step check accepted a first-derivative disagreement up to 10⁻⁸ absolute, which a cusp's rounding outgrew at large scale or far from the origin. The floor is now 10⁻⁸ of the stencil's least magnitude (each point measured by its larger coordinate), which bounds the rounding, plus |r″|, which a cusp at the origin keeps, both per unit of t. Where the stencil's magnitudes differ widely, the curve moves by about the largest within it, so its derivatives already exceed the largest's rounding. |r″| counts only where it agrees with the half step's to 10⁻², so a stencil that straddles a pole, whose sample lies 10⁻¹⁷ from it and is finite, has neither its own huge value nor its r″ to excuse the disagreement, and stays a gap at every scale. The second-derivative check adds the rounding of r″, 1024·u times that least magnitude over h², beside its fixed floor. A straight line 36,000 units out failed without it.
- The evolute and the probe treated |r′| < 10⁻⁹ as stationary. The evolute now uses 10⁻⁹(|p| + |r″|) and the probe 10⁻⁹(|p| + study radius), per unit of t. The probe leaves out r″, whose stencil value beside a pole would hide a tangent it can still show.
- A chord's endpoints coincided within 10⁻⁹(1 + |p| + |q|). The 1 made every chord of a 10⁻⁶ study near coincidence lose its neighbors. The test is now 10⁻⁹(|p| + |q|).
- A rolling curve's contact stopped Newton's method at a residual of 10⁻¹⁵(1 + ℓ) for a curve of length ℓ, so a tiny curve was placed to only 10⁻¹⁰ of its size. The stop is now 10⁻¹⁵(b + ℓ), with b the power of two at or below ℓ. That rounds as before for ℓ from 1 to 2.

Every preset's samples, warnings and diagnostics, with refinement on or off, are bit for bit what they were. The one change is the probe between samples on **Ellipse rolling on an ellipse**: its moving curve is about 10.2 long, so the contact's Newton stop moved from 1.1 × 10⁻¹⁴ to 1.8 × 10⁻¹⁴, and the probe's rolling point moved by about 10⁻¹³ of its coordinates.

**Constants that remain absolute.** The tested range does not reach these. Where |r′| < 10⁻⁹ there is no pedal, offset, involute, ray or caustic. A ray envelope's denominator has a tolerance of 10⁻⁹, a circle family's center is stationary below 10⁻⁹, and the second-derivative check's fixed floor is 10⁻⁴·max(1, |r′|/span). The flat test compares the cross product with 10⁻¹⁰|r′|·max(|r″|, 1). A chord's endpoint slack is 10⁻⁹(1 + length), and a rolling curve is closed within 10⁻⁹(1 + size). They hold between 2⁻²⁰ and 2¹³. Below about 10⁻⁶, some would begin to drop or keep samples that the same study at unit scale keeps or drops.

**Drawing.** The 2D drawing frames, fences and grids a study by its own size, so a copy draws the original's picture. Each family's outer Tukey fences sit three interquartile ranges beyond its quartiles, and along an axis where it hardly spreads, three fifths of its size: the power of two at or below its larger interquartile range, as the engine measures a study (`spreadUnit`). For a line 1 to 2 long, evenly sampled, that is 0.3, as it was at every scale before. The frame is the retained points' extent; an axis with none, as across a line, sets no limit. A drawing within 2⁻⁴⁰ of a point is framed 0.1 across. Page coordinates subtract the frame's center in double precision, and the grid's step is the power of ten at or below a fifth of the visible span. `tests/planar-rebase.spec.ts` maps every preset's result, with its probe and its refinement framed, by the five similarities above and draws both, fitted and zoomed in a thousandfold: every element, ray, glyph, probe mark and grid line agrees within 0.002 page units, and the grid stays evenly spaced at multiples of its step. Exports draw the same SVG. Every preset's framing is unchanged bit for bit, with refinement and the probe on or off.

## Spatial tangent developable

This construction belongs to `engine3`, with true vectors `(x,y,z)`. For coprime positive winding numbers p and q, major radius R and minor radius r with `0 < r < R`, define the torus knot

`c(t) = ((R+r cos qt) cos pt, (R+r cos qt) sin pt, r sin qt)`, `0 ≤ t ≤ 2π`.

Its speed squared is `p²(R+r cos qt)² + r²q² > 0`, so the unit tangent `T=c′/|c′|` exists throughout. With tangent reach L, the truncated tangent developable is `S(t,u)=c(t)+uT(t)` for `−L ≤ u ≤ L`. Each ruling has length 2L in world units. This is a ruled surface formed from the curve's tangents, not a tubular neighborhood of the knot. See the [Wolfram implementation of tangent developables](https://www.wolframcloud.com/obj/resourcesystem/published/FunctionRepository/resources/TangentDevelopableSurface/).

At u=0, `S_t=c′` and `S_u=T` are parallel: the knot is a singular seam, retained explicitly. Away from the seam and zero curvature, the normal is parallel to `c′ × c″`, constant along each ruling. Thus the regular sheets have zero Gaussian curvature. For the torus generator the engine computes first and second derivatives analytically, and uses normalized `±(c′ × c″)` for the two separately tessellated sheets; the renderer shades both sides. No triangles span from negative to positive u. Self-intersections remain part of the construction and depth testing reveals the visible sheets; no physical collision or optical simulation is implied.

Where both curvature and torsion are nonzero, the seam is a cuspidal edge: the two sheets meet tangentially in a sharp fold. At an isolated torsion zero the local picture changes to a cuspidal cross-cap, where the sheets cross instead of folding (Cleave, 1980), so the explanation says the fold holds "except where the curve momentarily stops twisting". Among the presets, the torsion of the trefoil changes sign 6 times and that of the (3, 4) woven orbit 8 times; the cinquefoil and the helix twist throughout. The spatial Lissajous curve `(2 sin(2t+π/2), 2 sin 3t, sin 5t)` has 4 torsion zeros and is stationary at t = π/2 and 3π/2, where all three velocity components vanish and no tangent exists; the engine's speed guard leaves gaps there.

If the cross product is too small relative to the analytic derivative scale, or adjacent normals reverse beyond 90 degrees, adjacent faces are omitted. This includes unresolved zero-curvature intervals between samples. The base, boundary curves, and straight rulings remain well-defined there. The closure sample copies the start exactly. Sampling uses 240–2400 intervals; boundary chord error converges quadratically. Uniform sampling and these guards are not certified adaptive tessellation. Very narrow folds can need a higher sample count.

Custom space curves use three bounded expressions `x(t)`, `y(t)`, and `z(t)` with a numerical `a` binding. Constants and functions follow the existing parser. Domain bounds lie within ±10⁶, with width between 10⁻⁶ and 10⁵. There is no implicit periodic closure: endpoints are evaluated independently. The same `S(t,u)` definition and unit-length tangent apply. Five-point Lagrange stencils stay within the domain (one-sided at endpoints), subtract the evaluation origin to reduce cancellation, and compare against half-step derivatives. Unstable first derivatives, a curve that [stands still](#spatial-scale-and-translation) (speed below 10⁻⁹ of its own size per unit of parameter), nonfinite samples, or coordinate magnitude at least 10¹² leave null gaps. Unstable second derivatives retain a valid tangent but omit surface faces. Relative normal thresholds are 10⁻⁸ for analytic generators and 10⁻⁶ for numerical derivatives; a straight line therefore retains its tangent construction without inventing a surface normal.

Midpoint checks, tangent reversals, and chord-versus-local-speed checks mark interval breaks even when both sampled endpoints are finite. A pole, cusp, or unresolved normal reversal never intentionally becomes a joining triangle. These are bounded numerical guards, not a proof that arbitrary expressions contain no features between samples; highly oscillatory curves can need more samples or a smaller domain. Tests compare custom helices and torus expressions with independent analytic tangents/normals and test reparameterization, straight lines, poles, cusps, and invalid inputs.

Camera fitting treats the base and each boundary as independent point families, rejects isolated asymptotic extremes using outer Tukey fences, and combines the retained extrema before computing a bounding sphere. A family's fences apply only where its quartiles are apart by more than their rounding, and its radius is its own however small (see [scale and translation](#spatial-scale-and-translation)). Reveal uses the same policy on the retained prefix. Fitting is heuristic; Hold current view and manual pan/zoom provide access to distant branches. Animation interpolates inputs only, never output triangles across changing singularities.

## Spatial involute

For a regular space curve r(t) with unit tangent `T = r′/|r′|`, choose an anchor t₀ inside the domain and a signed string length c. With the signed arc length `s(t) = ∫[t₀,t] |r′(v)| dv` (negative before the anchor), the involute is

`I_c(t) = r(t) + (c − s(t)) T(t)`.

Think of a string of length c held taut along the tangent at the anchor and unwound as the contact point moves: its free end traces I_c. The segment from r to I_c lies on the tangent line and has length `|c − s|`; these are the drawn **unwinding strings**. Since `dI_c/ds = (c − s) dT/ds` and `T · dT/ds = 0`, the filament's tangent is perpendicular to T wherever it is regular, so every filament crosses the strings at right angles. Where `s = c` the string has run out: the filament touches the curve and has a cusp. That point is kept, not rejected for its vanishing derivative. A straight line's involute is a single point (`r(t₀) + cT`), drawn as a small three-axis cross and reported as collapsed.

**Relation to the planar involute.** The planar notebook writes `I = r − (s + c_planar)T` with arc length from the domain start. With t₀ at the domain start the two agree when `c = −c_planar`; the spatial convention makes c the string's length at the anchor and places the cusp at `s = c`. The anchor and c are part of the study: changing t₀ changes every filament.

**Arc length.** Only a stable nonzero first derivative is needed, from the torus generator's analytic derivative or the bounded five-point stencils used for custom curves. Arc length uses Simpson's rule on each sample interval from the speeds at its ends and midpoint, so it converges at fourth order. The anchor's own interval is split at t₀ with Simpson's rule on each part. Accumulation proceeds outward from the anchor on both sides and stops at the first invalid sample or interval break (a pole, stationary point, tangent reversal, or chord inconsistent with the local speed, as in the developable); every regular sample beyond it is **unreached** and has no filament point, and a note counts them. Arc length is never carried across a gap. With **Restart after a break** (`involute.restart`, off unless asked) it starts again instead: every further stretch, a maximal run of intervals that carry arc length and that the anchor's does not reach, gets an anchor of its own halfway between the parameters of its first and last regular samples, with s = 0 there and the same string length c, and arc length is measured from it as from t₀. The restarted anchors are reported in increasing t (`restarts`) and named in the note. The middle moves with the sampling by at most half a sample spacing; the stretch's first sample past the break would put s = 0 next to an asymptote. A lone regular sample between two breaks stays unreached. The stretches meet only at breaks, so each member is still broken there, and between samples a member is never carried across one. An anchor outside the domain, at an invalid point, or in a broken interval is refused with an explicit error. On a closed torus knot the curve closes but its involutes generally do not: with the anchor at 0 the full length ℓ of the knot accumulates across the domain, so the last point is `r(2π) + (c − ℓ)T`, not the first.

**Families.** A family replaces c with `count` lengths `c_k = from·(1 − k/(count − 1)) + to·k/(count − 1)`, k = 0…count − 1, so both entered endpoints are exact and a descending range stays descending. Each member is exactly the single involute at its length. A family has 2–24 members and at most 48,000 points in total (members × (samples + 1)); single lengths and family endpoints lie within ±100000. At each representative sample one string runs along the tangent from `r + min(0, from − s, to − s)T` to `r + max(0, from − s, to − s)T`, reaching the curve and crossing every member. Members are indexed like the base samples and share its breaks, so reveal truncates the filaments measured on the final grid from the original anchor and never re-measures a prefix. Parameter animation recomputes the whole arc length in Go for each frame, including when t₀, c, the family range, or the count moves.

**Framing.** The base curve and each member are fitted as independent point families, using the same outer Tukey fences, before combining them, so a short base with a distant filament still frames the filament. Member color drifts from teal toward gold by position in the family; it is decorative and encodes no measured quantity.

**Checks.** On the helix `(2 cos t, 2 sin t, a t/3)`, `s = k(t − t₀)` with `k = √(4 + a²/9)`, and every involute lies in the plane `z = a t₀/3 + c a/(3k)`: an involute of a circle, lifted. Tests compare filaments with this closed form to 10⁻⁸, check `|I − r| = |c − s|`, retain the cusp at `s = c`, and match the planar engine's circle involute with `c = −c_planar`. They also check rigid motions and a regular reparameterization with corresponding anchors, fourth-order convergence of the knot's arc length against an analytic-speed reference, second-order decay of the central chord's component along T, collapsed lines, stopping at stationary points and poles on the far side of the anchor, refused anchors, exact family endpoints and strings that reach every member, independent framing of a short base, and the input bounds. The WebAssembly bridge test checks a helix family's planes and strings. With restart, on `(tan t, cos t, sin t)` over [−2, 2], every regular sample is reached, one anchor lies past each asymptote, the points past them match `r + (c − s)T` with s measured from that anchor by an independent fine Simpson sum to 10⁻⁷, the anchor's own stretch is unchanged bit for bit, and a study without restart reports none; across the cusp of `(t², t³)` the probe has no filament point between the two stretches but one on either side, and refinement draws past the asymptotes without crossing them.

## Spatial tangent-foot projection and orthotomic

For a regular space curve r(t), unit tangent T, and independent pole P, the perpendicular foot on the **tangent line** is `H = r + ((P − r)·T)T`. The **tangent-line orthotomic** is `Q = 2H − P`: the half-turn of P about that line. Indeed `H − r` is parallel to T, `(P − H)·T = 0`, and `Q − r = 2T(T·(P − r)) − (P − r)`, Rodrigues' rotation formula at angle π. H bisects P–Q, `|Q − H| = |P − H|`, and `|Q − r| = |P − r|`. With a planar curve and coplanar pole these reduce to the planar pedal and orthotomic. A space curve has a two-dimensional normal plane, so no unique spatial contrapedal or chosen Frenet normal is implied.

The independent pole's three coordinates lie within ±100000 and accept the shared constant-expression parser. Only a stable, nonzero **first derivative of the base** is required; an image cusp or collapsed image is retained. A straight line produces a fixed foot and a fixed half-turn image, drawn as a cross. For a circle about P the foot is the circle itself and the orthotomic has twice its radius. A pole on the curve stays fixed at that contact, including a cusp of the image. The construction is unchanged when T is reversed.

Sampling uses the spatial engine's existing first-derivative, midpoint, tangent-reversal, and chord guards. Base gaps and interval breaks are retained; projection resumes on the next regular component because it has no accumulated arc length. Nonfinite feet/images or coordinates of magnitude at least 10¹² become null image samples and are counted separately. The shared reversal guard can conservatively leave an interval open even if its tangent-line image has a continuous limiting extension; no tangent is invented at a stationary sample. Uniform sampling remains heuristic, not certified topology.

Representative constructions contain the contact r, foot H, and image (H or Q), with their original sample index. Draw r–H along the tangent and P–H perpendicular to it; in orthotomic mode also draw H–Q. Small crosses mark H and a larger cross marks P. The derived curve, connectors/feet, and pole marker have independent layers. Base, feet, derived path, and pole are fitted as separate families before combining robust bounds. Reveal slices paths and filters representative constructions on the final grid. Pole-coordinate animation recomputes the geometry in Go; all camera and media policies are shared with other spatial studies.

Native tests verify perpendicularity, the half-turn and distance identities against analytic knot tangents, line and circle images, a pole on the curve, planar-engine agreement, rigid motions, parameter reversal, stationary points and poles between samples, independent framing, input bounds, and finite serialization. The WASM bridge checks helix projections against analytic tangents and their sample correspondences.

## Spatial sphere inversion

Inversion in the sphere of center O and radius R > 0 is `J(p) = O + R²(p − O)/|p − O|²`. J lies on the ray from O through p with `|J − O| · |p − O| = R²`, so the sphere is fixed pointwise, inside and outside trade places, and `J(J(p)) = p`. O has no image; it corresponds to the point at infinity. Inversion maps spheres and planes to spheres and planes, so circles and lines map to circles and lines: a line or circle through O becomes a line or circle missing O, and conversely. It is conformal, scaling lengths near p by `R²/|p − O|²` and reversing orientation. Center coordinates lie within ±100000 and R is in (0, 100000]; all four accept the shared constant-expression parser.

The inverted **source** is the base curve or one of its tangent projections (tangent-foot curve or tangent-line orthotomic) from the independent pole. A derived source is evaluated pointwise from the base's position and unit tangent at each sample and interval midpoint, exactly as the projection itself is, never by differentiating a sampled path. Composition is limited to this one level; involute filaments are not an input, since they need arc length from an anchor. The inversion center is independent of the projection pole.

**Passages through the center.** The image of a continuous source that passes through O runs off to infinity and returns from the opposite direction, so the two sides must never be joined. A source sample exactly at O, or whose image has a coordinate of magnitude at least 10¹², is a null image sample, counted as invalid. Between samples, each interval also inverts its midpoint source point M: if the image turns back, `(J(M) − J(rᵢ))·(J(rᵢ₊₁) − J(M)) < 0`, or J(M) is undefined, the interval is broken and counted as a passage. Along a line through O, for example, J moves monotonically toward −∞ on one side of O and in from +∞ on the other, so any chord across O points against the motion. Half-chords below 10⁻⁹(R + |J(M) − O|) are ignored, so a collapsed image is not broken by roundoff. A near passage whose small loop the samples cannot resolve is broken in the same way rather than joined with a chord; more samples resolve it, and so does [refining between samples](#spatial-refinement-between-samples), which then decides every passage. The image keeps every base gap and interval break as well.

Correspondence segments join a source point to its image along their common ray from O. Three great circles in the coordinate planes and a cross at O mark the sphere without a surface that would hide the curves. The base, source, image, and center are framed as separate families with robust bounds, so a tiny source near O and its enormous image, or a distant source and its tiny image near O, both stay in view. Reveal slices the paths and image breaks and filters correspondences by sample; animating the center or radius recomputes the image in Go, and the camera and media policies match other spatial studies.

**Checks.** Native tests verify pointwise involution, collinearity and `|OJ||Op| = R²` on a torus knot, and the analytic cases: a line missing O becomes the circle through O with the expected center and radius; circles in and off O's plane become circles; a circle through O becomes the line `x = R²/2` with exactly one break at the passage; a line through O maps to itself, broken once between samples; a sample at O, or beside it with an oversized image, is a gap. Inverting the image of a circle recovers the circle. They also check rigid motions, a common scale of curve, center, and radius, parameter reversal, derived inputs against the projection engine, a collapsed image from a line's tangent feet, retained base gaps, independent framing, input bounds, and finite serialization. The WebAssembly bridge test checks the circle-through-center case and an orthotomic source.

## Spatial constructions on a derived curve

The tangent developable, involute, framed ribbon, ruled surface and canal surface act on a curve g, chosen by **Built on** (`input`): the base curve r itself, its tangent-foot curve `g = H = r + ((P − r)·T)T` or tangent-line orthotomic `g = Q = 2H − P` from the pole P of the projections above, one of its involutes `g = I = r + (c − s)T` (see [Built on an involute](#built-on-an-involute) below), or a coil `g = r + dD` that winds around it (see [Built on a coil](#built-on-a-coil)). Every formula of those constructions then reads g, its unit tangent, arc length, Frenet frame and curvature in place of r's: the involute is `I = g + (c − s_g)T_g`, the developable `g + uT_g`, the tube the envelope of spheres centered on g, and a chord pairs g(t) with g(mt + δ). The tangent-foot and orthotomic constructions, sphere inversion (which has its own input, above) and the curve alone ignore the choice. Composition is one level deep: a derived curve of a derived curve is not offered.

**Evaluation.** g is evaluated pointwise from the base's own position and unit tangent at any t, never from a sampled path, and differentiated numerically by the five-point stencils of an expression curve (spacing `domain_span × 10⁻⁴`, checked against half that step). An expression base keeps its own stencil beneath it: widening the base's step (as the planar notebook does) was measured to raise the composed helix's curvature error from 2·10⁻⁷ to 3·10⁻⁶ and its torsion error from 2·10⁻⁴ to 3·10⁻³, since the base's truncation error grows as the fourth power of its step while its roundoff stays far below it. Torus knots and harmonic curves keep their analytic r and r′ beneath g. Because g's derivatives are numerical, its tolerances are those of an expression curve, and its third and fourth derivatives (torsion, and the ruled surface's probe) come from differencing at a fixed step: they may be reported unknown where two steps disagree, and carry an error of a few percent at sharp bends.

**Cusps.** With κ, N the base's curvature and normal, `dH/ds = κ[(P − r)·N]T + κ[(P − r)·T]N`, so the tangent-foot curve moves at speed `κ|Π(P − r)|`, Π the projection onto the osculating plane: it stops where the base is flat (κ = 0) or where P lies on the binormal line through r(t). The orthotomic moves at twice that speed and stops at the same places. In space a pole generically lies on no binormal line, so a derived curve is usually regular; a pole on the curve itself (P = r(t₀)) always stops it there, in a cusp at the pole, and a base that straightens toward an asymptote slows its foot to a stop as well. Each foot lies on the sphere with diameter from r(t) to P, so g is finite wherever the base is and has no asymptote of its own; it is broken at every break of the base (a gap, a pole between samples, a cusp of the base), as the projections and inversion are, even where the tangent line and its foot continue across a cusp of the base. Where g stops or turns back, the sampling loop's existing guards (a vanishing derivative, a reversed tangent across an interval, a chord its midpoint velocity cannot explain) break it, and every construction on it is broken there rather than joined across: arc length stops, no face spans the interval, and a frame restarts. The note counts each run of such intervals where the base itself continues, except at an open curve's ends, where there is nothing to join across; a closed curve's ends meet, so a stop there is one cusp. A pole that leaves g no regular sample (a line seen from a point off it, whose tangent-foot curve is one point) is refused with a message naming the input curve.

**Drawing.** The drawing treats g as its curve: the probe describes g, the reveal and the representative lines follow g's samples, and the legend names it. The base curve, its breaks, and representative connectors from base point to tangent foot (and on to the orthotomic point), with the pole, are drawn in gray as their own layers and framed with the study. Parameter animation offers the pole after the construction's own tracks.

**Checks.** Native tests verify: the unit circle's tangent-foot curve from a point on it is the cardioid `(cos t − sin²t, sin t (1 + cos t), 0)`, with one cusp at the pole, broken there for the involute, developable and framed ribbon while the base stays unbroken; an involute anchored at t₀ = 0 unwinds the cardioid's arc length `4 sin(t/2)`, each string lying along the cardioid's tangent and crossing the involute at right angles, and stops at the cusp; the helix `(2 cos t, 2 sin t, t/2)` has the analytic tangent-foot curve `r − (b²t/w²)W` and developable rulings along its analytic tangent; constructions on the orthotomic equal those on the tangent-foot curve scaled by 2 about the pole when their world lengths double too; a tube around the tangent-foot curve and orthotomic of a circle from its center is the torus of core radius 2 or 4; translating base and pole together translates the result; the base input and every construction that takes no input leave results exactly unchanged; diagnostics give g's own tangent and osculating centers, and the composed helix's curvature and torsion match their analytic values to 10⁻⁶ and 10⁻³; on a knot's orthotomic, torsion is the input curve's own binormal turning rate; an open domain ending at the cusp counts none and the closed harmonic loop one; the base beneath the input is sampled and broken exactly as the same study built on the base, including a pole a quarter of the way into an interval that only the chord guard finds, and every base break, including a cusp of the base, breaks the input; the base and pole are inside the study's bounds. The WASM bridge repeats the cardioid case.

### Built on an involute

With `input` set to `involute`, g is the involute `I(t) = r(t) + (c − s(t))T(t)` of the [spatial involute](#spatial-involute), with its own anchor t₀ and signed string length c (`unwinding`; **Input anchor t₀** and **Input string c**). They are separate from the involute construction's anchor and length, so an involute can be unwound from an involute. The pole is neither used, validated nor framed. c lies within ±100000; t₀ must be finite, inside the domain, and on a regular, unbroken interval of the base, or the study is refused with a message naming the input's anchor.

**Geometry.** Since `r′ = |r′|T` and `s′ = |r′|`, `I′ = (c − s)T′ = (c − s)|r′|κN`: the involute's tangent is the base's principal normal, so a tangent developable built on it is ruled along the base's normals, and `(r − I)·I′ = 0` puts the base point in the involute's normal plane. Every string r − I is normal to the involute, so the base is one of the involute's evolutes; in the plane that evolute is unique, in space it is one of infinitely many lying on the involute's polar developable. The involute stops where the string runs out (s = c, a cusp) and wherever the base is straight (κ = 0, where N is undefined and reverses across a simple inflection): both are breaks, counted as cusps where the base continues. On the helix `(a cos t, a sin t, bt)`, `w = √(a² + b²)`, the involute lies in the plane `z = bt₀ + bc/w` as an involute of the circle of radius a, with curvature `w/(a|c − s|)` and no torsion. A closed base has an open involute: unwound from one end it ends a whole length ℓ of string away, `I(end) − I(start) = −ℓT`, so constructions on it treat the domain as open.

**Evaluation.** Arc length is tabulated at the sample parameters outward from t₀, with eight-point Gauss–Legendre quadrature of `|r′|` on each interval (the anchor's interval split at t₀), and stops at the first missing sample or break of the base on either side, as the involute construction does. At any other t it is completed by four-point Gauss–Legendre quadrature from the nearer tabulated sample, at most half an interval away; it is never summed along a drawn path. Samples beyond a base break are **unreached**: they have no involute point, are counted in the note, and are not cusps. With **Restart input after a break** (`unwinding.restart`) the input's arc length starts again on each further stretch, anchored as the involute construction's restarts are (see **Arc length** under [Spatial involute](#spatial-involute)) and reported as the composition's `restarts`; each stretch's string then runs out where its own s = c, so each can add a cusp. The derivatives come from the base's own rather than from differencing I: `I′ = (c − s)T′` with `T′ = (r″ − (r″·T)T)/|r′|` from the base's r″, and `I″ = −|r′|T′ + (c − s)T″`, with T″ differenced from T at spacing `domain_span × 10⁻⁴` and discarded where half that step disagrees, as for an expression curve. A base sample with `|r″ − (r″·T)T| ≤ 10⁻⁶ max(|r″|, |r′|/domain_span)` is straight and gives no involute sample; a base straight wherever arc length reaches (a line) leaves nothing to build on and is refused. Third and fourth derivatives of I (torsion, and the probes) are differenced as for the projections.

**Drawing and animation.** The base is drawn in gray beneath the involute with representative strings from r to I along its tangents (the **Strings from the base** layer, whose foot is the base point itself); there is no pole marker. Parameter animation offers **Input string c** and **Input anchor t₀** after the construction's own tracks, recomputing the arc length in Go for each frame. Reveal slices the base, strings and involute on the final grid.

**Workload.** Each involute evaluation costs one base evaluation, four for the partial arc length and ten for T″. With every probe on at the 2400-sample cap, a developable built on a knot expression's involute took about 1.0 s natively, against 0.74 s for the same study on its tangent-foot curve and 58 ms on the base; an involute family on it took 0.26 s.

**Checks (involute input).** Native tests verify the helix's involute against its closed form to 10⁻⁹, its plane, one cusp, rulings along the helix's inward normal, the base in each normal plane, and strings from base to involute; its curvature `w/(a|c − s|)` to 10⁻⁵ and vanishing torsion through the probe; on the analytic trefoil, strings along the knot's tangents, rulings along its probe's principal normals and the knot in each normal plane, and the open end `−ℓT` against an independent arc length; the parabola's involute against the closed-form arc length `(t√(1 + t²) + asinh t)/2` to 10⁻⁹ at 240 samples with the anchor between samples, and under a regular reparameterization; unreached samples beyond the asymptotes of `(tan t, cos t, sin t)`, counted apart from its one cusp; translation; the involute of the helix's involute against `s₂ = (a/w)∫(c − s)dt` along the inward normal; every construction acting on the involute, with a far invalid pole neither refused nor framed; unwinding ignored by the other inputs; and refusal of anchors outside the domain or beyond a break, out-of-range lengths, and a straight base. The WASM bridge checks the helix case. With restart, the same curve's input is reached at every regular sample, matches the involute anchored past each asymptote to 10⁻⁷ away from where each stretch's string runs out, has three cusps, and leaves the anchor's stretch unchanged; the semicubical parabola's other side is unwound from one anchor before its cusp.

### Built on a coil

With `input` set to `coil`, g is a coil of the base, `g(t) = r(t) + d D(t)`, with `D = cos θ U + sin θ V` in the base's rotation-minimizing frame and `θ(s) = θ₀ + 2πN s/L`, s the arc length and L its total (`coil`; **Coil radius d**, **Coil angle θ₀**, **Coil turns**). It is the framed construction's offset strand, with settings of its own, so a ribbon, a tube's meridians or strands can turn about a coil on their own terms: a coil of a coil. The frame starts from e_z projected onto the normal plane where each unbroken stretch begins (or the least aligned axis where e_z is tangent), so θ₀ is measured from there; a different start would only turn each stretch by a constant angle. On an unbroken closed loop the frame's holonomy α is always distributed along it, `−α s/L`, so the coil closes when N is a whole number, and the constructions on it then treat the domain as closed; otherwise it ends turned away from where it began, and they treat it as open. d lies in [0, 100000], θ₀ within ±1000 radians and N within ±100 turns. The pole is neither used, validated nor framed.

**Geometry.** With σ = |r′|, `T′ = (r″ − (r″·T)T)/σ` and `ω = (2πN + C)/L`, C the distributed correction, the transported frame obeys `U′ = −(U·T′)T` and `V′ = −(V·T′)T`, so

  `D′ = ωσ T×D − (D·T′)T`,  `g′ = r′ + d D′ = σ[(1 − dκ D·N) T + dω T×D]`.

A turning coil (ω ≠ 0, d > 0) is therefore regular everywhere: its velocity always has the component dωσ along T×D. With no turns, it is a parallel curve along a transported normal, which stops in a cusp wherever dκ(D·N) = 1, as the parallel curve of an ellipse does where d crosses its radius of curvature. On the unit circle, whose transported normal is e_z, the coil is the torus curve `(1 + d sin θ)(cos t, sin t, 0) + d cos θ e_z`, θ = θ₀ + Nt.

**Evaluation.** The frame and arc length are tabulated at the base's samples exactly as the framed construction tabulates them (transport by double reflection, Simpson arc length per interval, a restart after every break), so at the samples the coil input is the framed construction's offset strand with the same d, θ₀ and N (its offset, angle and twist), e_z as N₀ and the correction distributed. At any other t it is completed from the sample at or before t by one transport step and the Simpson step over part of the interval, as the framed construction's strands are refined, so the coil passes through its samples. The derivatives are written from the base's own and the transport law rather than by differencing g:

  `D″ = ω(r″·T) T×D + ωσ T′×D − (ωσ)²D − (ωσ (T×D)·T′ + D·T″)T − (D·T′)T′`,  `g″ = r″ + d D″`,

with only T″ differenced from T at spacing `domain_span × 10⁻⁴` and discarded where half that step disagrees. Third and fourth derivatives (torsion, and the probes) are differenced as for the other inputs. The coil breaks wherever the base does, and every regular sample has a coil point: there is nothing unreached.

**Drawing and animation.** The base is drawn in gray beneath the coil with representative **Coil arms** from r to g, each d long; there is no pole marker. Parameter animation offers **Coil radius d**, **Coil angle θ₀** and **Coil turns** after the construction's own tracks. **A coiled cord round a trefoil** builds a tube on a coil that winds 36 times round the trefoil, which closes with it, and winds the turns up from 0 when played; **Threads twisted round a coiled helix** twists three framed strands round a coil that winds round a helix.

**Workload.** Each coil evaluation costs one base evaluation and ten more for T″. A framed construction's three strands refined between samples on a coil at 2400 samples took about 1.0 s natively, against 0.1 s without refinement; a tube on the cord's coil at 2400 samples took 35 ms, and writing its mesh as JSON about three times that, so the cord's preset uses 960 samples, which give its tube the same 480 rings.

**Checks (coil input).** Native tests verify the circle's coil against its closed form to 10⁻¹² at the samples and 10⁻⁹ between them, with g′ and g″ against the closed form's derivatives; that a whole number of turns closes the coil (no broken interval) and a half turn leaves it open; the samples against the framed construction's offset strand on a closed knot (with its correction), an open stretch of it, across the asymptotes of `(tan t, cos t, sin t)`, across a cusp between samples, and on a closed harmonic astroid whose four cusps break the loop, so that no correction applies; g′ and g″ against central differences of g on an expression knot, an analytic knot and an open curve; the four cusps of an ellipse's untwisted inner coil and none once it is twisted; restarts from e_z after each asymptote, with no sample unreached; every construction acting on the coil, with a far invalid pole neither refused nor framed and a closed tube round the closed coil; translation; refinement onto a coil of forty turns within 1.5 tolerances; the setting ignored by the other inputs; and the bounds, and refusal of a coil that stands still. The WASM bridge checks the circle's coil and its closed tube.

## Spatial refinement between samples

Every spatial curve is sampled at evenly spaced parameters `tᵢ = t₀ + i(t₁ − t₀)/n` and drawn as the chords joining them. A chord can stray far from the curve: across a tight fold, around a loop thrown out by inversion near its center, or over a coil faster than the samples, which they alias into a slow wave that is not there. **Refine between samples** (`adaptive`) inserts drawn points between the samples where a chord strays. The samples stay the study's identity: its surfaces, construction lines, reveal and probe all keep the evenly spaced samples, and so does framing unless the study [frames its points between samples](#framing-between-samples); every sample is present, unchanged, in the refined path.

**Error and tolerance.** A piece [a, b] of a sample interval is judged at three probes, the curve's points at its quarter points. Its error is the largest distance from a probe to the chord between its ends, the true distance to the segment rather than to the line, so a curve that doubles back is measured too. The tolerance is ε = 2 × 10⁻⁴ of the radius fitted to that curve's own samples by the robust fit used for framing, so refinement does not depend on the curve's scale or position: scaling a curve by a power of two inserts exactly the same points. Pieces are bisected worst first. Each bisection draws the old midpoint and gives each half its own three probes, two of them new; a piece the scan below has probed more densely passes its probes to its halves instead. On a smooth curve a chord of length ℓ strays by about κℓ²/8, so each bisection cuts the error by about four, and the measured error follows the tolerance down.

Since framing's radius is the curve's own, without a floor (see [scale and translation](#spatial-scale-and-translation)), this holds for a curve of any size.

**Bounds.** Each sample interval is bisected at most 10 times, so no drawn point lies closer to its neighbor than 1/1024 of a sample step, and at most 16,384 points are added to one curve. When the budget runs out the remaining error is still spread evenly, since the worst pieces went first. Pieces still above the tolerance at either limit are counted as unresolved and reported. The scan below adds at most 4,096 probes to a curve, and draws points only where it finds something.

**Breaks between samples.** A piece with an undefined probe (a nonfinite point, or one beyond 10¹² from the origin) is bisected toward it until the finest step, so the drawn curve stops within one finest step of where the curve stops. A piece still longer than 50 tolerances at the finest step cannot belong to a continuous curve: the curve jumps there, through a pole. Either way the sample interval is broken, and the break is reported. The interval also breaks every surface built on the curve, so no face is joined across a gap the samples missed. Refinement never joins an interval the uniform sampling breaks: across a pole, stationary point or cusp the guards of the [tangent developable](#spatial-tangent-developable) still stand.

**Narrow features between probes.** Three probes see nothing of a feature that falls between them: a window where the curve is undefined, a pole whose flanks stay within the tolerance at the probes, a spike, or a sharp turn close to a sample, such as a caustic's cusp. Once every piece is within tolerance, a scan probes the pieces more densely, a level at a time. Each level halves the spacing of every piece's probes, from 1/8 of a sample step down to the finest step, and is taken only if the whole level fits within 4,096 more probes for the curve. So a curve of at most 1,024 sample intervals is scanned to at least 1/8 of a step, of at most 341 to 1/16, 146 to 1/32 and 68 to 1/64, while one of more than 1,024 is not scanned. A scanned piece returns to refinement when a probe is undefined, or when its probes stray beyond the tolerance and more than twice as far as its three quarter points: the scan saw something they could not. Refinement then goes on as above, judging each piece by all its probes, within the same depth and budget. Where the three probes already measure the curve, a denser probe strays little farther than they do, and the scan adds nothing to the drawing. The levels depend only on the sample count, and the decisions only on distances compared with the tolerance, so scaling a curve by a power of two still inserts exactly the same points. A spike whose flank climbs more than 50 tolerances within the finest step is broken there, as a pole is.

**Which curves.** Refinement draws the curve a construction is built on, the base curve beneath a [derived input](#spatial-constructions-on-a-derived-curve), a [tangent-foot curve or orthotomic](#spatial-tangent-foot-projection-and-orthotomic), a [sphere inversion](#spatial-sphere-inversion)'s image, each member of an [involute](#spatial-involute) family, whether drawn or built on, each [offset strand](#spatial-frames-offsets-and-ribbons) of a framed construction, a [ruled surface](#spatial-ruled-surfaces-and-chord-families)'s partner thread, and a [canal surface](#spatial-canal-surfaces-and-tubes)'s meridians. Each is evaluated pointwise at the probes, a custom curve from its expressions alone, a projection or inversion from the base's position and unit tangent, an involute, strand or meridian as described next, and a partner thread at φ(t) = mt + δ as at a sample, never by interpolating drawn points. The ribbon, contact circles, harmonic vector sums and ellipses, and every surface keep the evenly spaced samples, unless the surface is [refined along the curve](#surfaces-along-the-curve) too. A vector field's trajectories and a chase are integrated with their own adaptive steps and are not refined again. Surface patches, mirrors and level sets ignore the setting.

**Involutes between samples.** An involute input is evaluated between samples by its own evaluator: the arc length tabulated at the samples, completed to t by four-point Gauss–Legendre quadrature from the nearer sample (see [Built on an involute](#built-on-an-involute)). Its position shares one compiled function with the sample loop, so a refined input passes exactly through its samples, and it is undefined wherever a sample there would be: where t is unreached, or the base is undefined or straight. A member of the involute construction is `r + (c − s)T` at t between samples t_j and t_{j+1}, with s the sample's own arc length s(t_j) carried on by Simpson's rule on the speed over [t_j, t], from its ends and midpoint: the step the samples take, over part of an interval. So it converges at fourth order, as at the samples, continues each sample's value without a seam, and adds an error far below the samples' accumulated one. Refinement runs only between samples that both have a point, so it never continues past a stop of the arc length, and each member is refined and broken on its own. A gap the base's refinement finds breaks the base before the arc length is measured, so it stops there as at any break. Near a corner that is nearly sharp, where the tangent turns half a turn within a few samples, the free end of a string swings round a half circle of radius |c − s|, which the samples draw as a few chords.

**Strands between samples.** An offset strand `r + dD` is evaluated at t between samples t_i and t_{i+1} from the curve's own point and tangent there and the frame at t_i. A rotation-minimizing U is carried from t_i to t by one double reflection, the step the samples take, over part of an interval; V = T × U. The arc length is s(t_i) carried on to t by the Simpson step on the speed over [t_i, t], as for an involute, and sets θ(s), so the twist turns D smoothly between samples. A distributed correction turns the frame by its share of that arc, `−α(s − s(t_i))/L` beyond the turn already applied at t_i. A Frenet frame is the curve's own at t, U = B × T, undefined where the curvature vanishes, as at a sample. At t_{i+1} each of these is the sample's own step, so a strand continues its samples without a seam, and between samples it follows the frame the samples converge to. Refinement runs only between samples the frame joins: never across a break, a Frenet flip or a restart from N₀, and each strand is refined and broken on its own. The ribbon keeps the evenly spaced samples, so its edges stay on its mesh; refined along the curve, its edges are refined with it. A strand twisted N times turns about the curve every L/N of arc length; at fewer than about ten samples a turn the samples draw each turn as a few chords, and refining draws it round.

**Partner threads between samples.** A ruled surface's partner b(φ(t)) is evaluated pointwise at φ(t) = mt + δ, by the same evaluator as at the samples: a chord partner wraps round a closed curve's period and is missing outside an open domain, and a second thread is missing where it or its derivative is not finite. Refinement runs only between samples the surface joins, and its tolerance is 1/5000 of the radius fitted to the thread's own samples, not the base's. A gap or jump it finds between samples breaks the partner and the surface there. The surface, its rulings and its probe keep the evenly spaced samples, so a refined thread can lie just outside the mesh's edge, by at most that edge's chord error, unless the surface is refined along the curve too. A partner that winds k times as fast as the base, through the rate m or its own expressions, has k times fewer samples a turn; refining draws it round.

**Meridians between samples.** A canal's meridian k, `c + aT + ρD` with D at θ₀ + 2πk/M, is evaluated at t between samples from the curve's own point and velocity there, the profile's own radius R and slope R′ there, which set the contact circle's axial offset a = −RR′/v and radius ρ = R√(1 − R′²/v²), and the canal's rotation-minimizing frame carried to t as for a strand. So a meridian continues its samples without a seam, and between samples it follows the profile rather than an interpolation of the samples' circles. It is missing where the sphere or its contact circle is not real, |R′| > v. Each meridian is refined between the samples the canal joins, to a tolerance of 1/5000 of the radius fitted to its own samples. A gap or jump one finds between samples, such as an envelope lost in a window too narrow for the samples and the midpoint check, breaks the surface there, and every meridian that did not find it is refined again with that break; each path's own count of breaks reports only what it found. The circle at t is evaluated once for all the meridians probed there. The mesh, contact circles, folds and probe keep the evenly spaced samples, so a refined meridian can stand off the mesh by at most its chord error between rings, unless the surface is refined along the curve too. A meridian twisted N times winds round the tube every L/N of arc length; at fewer than about ten samples a turn, refining draws it round.

**Passages through the center of inversion.** With refinement on, a sphere inversion's image decides each passage through the center by refinement instead of by the midpoint test. A near miss at distance δ throws out a loop about R²/δ across, which the evenly spaced samples cut with a few long chords and may break; refined, it is drawn round and joined. A true passage sends the image off to infinity, so at the finest step its chord is still a jump, and the interval breaks and is counted as a passage, as before.

**Limits.** The scan can still miss a feature narrower than its spacing, and does not run on a curve of more than 1,024 sample intervals, so refinement is a heuristic, not certified topology. Raising the sample count still helps there. Unless the study frames its points between samples, framing uses the samples alone, so a feature that the samples miss entirely can leave the frame. The tolerance is set in the world, not on the screen: zooming in far enough shows the chords, and the drawing is the same whatever the view, in playback and in every export, unless the study is [refined to its view](#refinement-to-the-view).

**Checks.** Native tests measure the refined chords' actual distance from an independent formula of a torus knot (within 1.5 tolerances, against 2.6 tolerances uniformly). They also halve the tolerance from 10⁻² to 10⁻⁵, observing the measured error fall under each value. They resolve a coil of frequency 61 that 240 samples alias, and reach the top of a bump narrower than a sample step. They find a gap between samples, break the developable there and draw to within one finest step of each edge, and find a gap a tenth of a step wide between an interval's quarter point and midpoint, which its three probes miss, and break the developable there too. The shared package's own tests find a spike, an undefined window and a pole between the probes of a straight line, a bump beside a sample, and a spike 1.5 tolerances high on a wave its quarter points measure at 0.5; a circle, an aliased coil, and a wave whose crests stray 1.6% beyond its quarter points refine exactly as without the scan; scaling by 1024 inserts the same points; and the scan's probes are bounded, spent in whole levels, and stop when the budget runs out. They draw a line's inverted near miss as its exact circle with every chord's sagitta within tolerance, and keep a true passage broken. They also check an exhausted, reported and deterministic budget, scale invariance, the orthotomic against an independent formula, derived inputs, and that a smooth study's samples, breaks and surfaces are unchanged. For involutes they check a helix's family against its unwound circles, every refined point within 10⁻⁸ and every chord within 1.5 tolerances; a trefoil's involute through its cusp against an independent Gauss–Legendre arc length (itself checked on a circle and by additivity), with points between samples no farther from it than the samples are; a member stopped at a pole, with nothing drawn past it; a helix's involute input against its closed form; and an involute of that input against its closed form, a circle's involute's involute. For strands they check a twisted circle's strands against their closed form, every refined point within 10⁻⁹ and every chord within 1.5 tolerances where the uniform chords stray by more than four; a trefoil's strands under a rotation-minimizing frame with its seam shown or its correction distributed and under a Frenet frame, against the same study at eight times the samples, with points between samples no farther from it than the samples are; strands stopped at a pole, where the frame restarts, and at a Frenet flip that the curve does not share, with nothing drawn across either; and that a study's samples are unchanged. For partner threads they check a ring run thirty times as fast against its closed form, every refined point within 10⁻⁹ and every chord within 1.5 tolerances where the uniform chords stray by more than four, with a tolerance set by the thread's own size; a torus knot's chords five times as far round against the knot, wrapped round its period; chords that leave an open helix's domain, with nothing refined past the last; a thread undefined on a window narrower than a quarter interval, which the uniform study misses and refinement breaks, with no face across it; a thread's pole, with nothing inserted across it; and that a study's samples are unchanged. For meridians they check a torus's meridians twisted thirty times against their closed form, every refined point within 10⁻⁹ and every chord within 1.5 tolerances where the uniform chords stray by more than four, with the study otherwise unchanged; a cylinder's helical meridians, with a tolerance set by their own size rather than the axis's; a surface of revolution of varying radius against its closed contact circles; a knot's tube of varying radius, its seam shown or its correction distributed, against the same study at eight times the samples, with points between samples no farther from it than the samples are; a profile undefined, and an envelope lost, on windows narrower than a quarter interval, which the uniform study misses and refinement breaks in every meridian and the surface; and circles already lost at the samples, with nothing inserted there. The WebAssembly bridge test checks the near miss's circle, a refined helix involute family on its unwound circles, a twisted circle's refined strand on its coil, a fast ring's refined partner thread on its ring, and a twisted torus's refined meridians on their windings.

### Framing between samples

The bounds are fitted to the samples, each family within outer Tukey fences of its own samples, so a spike or loop narrower than a sample step, which refinement draws, can run off the page. With **Frame the points between samples** on (`adaptiveFrame`, with refinement), each refined curve also carries its _reach_ (`reach`, with positions `reachAt`), as in the [2D notebook](#refinement-between-samples): its points between samples outside the box of their interval's two samples, the only ones that can widen the samples' bounds (`refine.Reach`, shared by both engines). Go's fit (`fitReach`) adds each reach to the family drawn from that curve: the base, the curve beneath a derived input, a projection, an inversion's image, each involute, a ribbon's edges and strands, a ruled partner and a canal's meridians. A reach point joins only where it lies within its family's fences on every axis, so an excursion toward an asymptote is left out as an outlying sample is. The reach is always that of the curve refined to its own tolerance: under a view or a flight's piece, each curve is refined a second time without it, and since a view never moves a break the two refinements see the same breaks, so zooming in never moves the camera. A reveal fits the same families (`fitFamilies`) with the reach it has reached. Without the setting nothing is computed and the result is unchanged byte for byte.

**Checks.** Native tests (`engine3/reach_test.go`): a bump of height 1 in z between samples that see 0.021 of it, its top reached within 5 × 10⁻⁴ and the bounds centered halfway up it, with the reach exactly the refined points outside their intervals' boxes and nothing else in the result changed; a needle 2 long framed, and one 50 long left out beyond the fence; the reach and bounds identical byte for byte under three views and a flight's piece; every refined curve of eight constructions reporting its reach, each changing the bounds; and the reach of every preset mapped by the similarities of [scale and translation](#spatial-scale-and-translation). In the browser, **A dandelion clock** frames all sixty seed tips on the page at radius 2.2, where the samples alone frame a radius under 1.05 and leave about half of them off it.

### Refinement to the view

With **Refine to the view as you zoom** on, a drawing zoomed in past its fitted framing sends Go what it shows (`view`, `engine3.View`), and every refined curve is refined to it as well as to its own tolerance, through the shared focus of the [2D refinement to the view](#refinement-between-samples) (`refine.RefineFocused`). A view is the point c in the middle of the page, the page's unit directions right R and up U, and its half width W and half height H on the plane through c facing the viewer, read from the drawn camera's own arithmetic (`refinementView` in `web/spatial/scene.ts`). A perspective adds the eye's distance E behind c, looking along F = U × R, and the near distance N within which nothing is drawn. A point p is drawn at `R·(p − c)/(W w)` across and `U·(p − c)/(H w)` up, within ±1 on the page, where `w = 1 + F·(p − c)/E` is its distance ahead of the eye over E (w = 1 throughout an orthographic view).

**Meeting the view.** A piece meets the view when the box of its end points and probes, grown on every side by its largest probe's distance from the chord, reaches the view's margin, the page grown by half its width and height on each side: unless all eight of the box's corners lie beyond one of the margin's planes, `x = ±2w`, `y = ±2w`, or, in a perspective, `w = N/E`. Each is a plane in space, so a box beyond one misses the margin.

**Tolerance with depth.** A length ℓ at depth w is drawn ℓ/w as long as on the plane through c. So a piece that meets the view is refined to `2 × 10⁻⁴` of the view's half diagonal √(W² + H²), and to chords no longer than a quarter of it, each multiplied by the piece's depth: w at its nearest end point or probe, but never less than N/E, so a piece reaching behind the eye is refined as the nearest drawn depth needs. A piece whose tolerance is then no finer than its curve's own is refined as without the view, so far pieces in a deep perspective, and every piece of a study shown no closer than its own scale, are untouched, and a view that shows nothing finer gives the study's result to the byte. Pieces that meet the view are refined first. As in 2D, breaks are judged against the curve's own tolerance alone, so the view never moves a break, and the depth limit (1/1024 of a sample step) still bounds how far it can follow. The readout's **finer in view** is the curve's own tolerance over the finest tolerance of a piece in view.

**In the page.** The view is sent once the drawing has rested for a fifth of a second, and the drawing is busy until its curves arrive, so an export waits for them. Bounds, framing and surfaces' grids come from the samples, or from the curves refined to their own tolerance when the study frames its points between samples, so refining to the view never moves the camera; a surface [refined along the curve](#surfaces-along-the-curve) gains columns where its curves gain points, in view as elsewhere.
