The drawing counts layers on a target of the page's size, one sample a pixel, with its own depth buffer: the lines are drawn there depth only, as they are drawn behind sheets (so a dash's gaps are left out), keeping the nearest line at each pixel; then every sheet fragment that passes the depth test against that line, pushed back by the polygon offset, adds 1/255 to the target's alpha with additive blending and no depth writes, so the count is exact up to 255 and independent of drawing order. When the hidden lines are drawn, each fragment reads n at its pixel. A pixel takes the nearest line's count, so where two hidden lines cross on the page the farther takes the nearer's for that pixel. A pixel the count missed, at an outline the multisampled canvas reaches but the single sample does not, has n = 0 and is drawn as behind one. The target is 8-bit RGBA with a depth-and-stencil renderbuffer (typically 24 and 8 bits), about 8 bytes a pixel or 24 MB at 2000 × 1520, made only while lines fade this way. A page larger than the device's texture or renderbuffer limit is refused by name, as see-through pages are. Exports record `fade: "each"` and state the rule only when hidden lines are drawn and fade this way.

The drawing tests lines against sheets alone: they are drawn after the sheets and before the visible lines, without writing depth, so a line never hides a line.

The visible-only line drawing applies the same rules on the CPU. Its sampled visibility test already splits each segment into visible and hidden runs, with transitions halfway between disagreeing samples; hidden runs go to their own groups. Dashes there are cut at the exact parameters where `s·k` crosses an integer or an integer plus 0.55, with `s` carried from the segment's ends through the cut's clipping and the page clipping, both of which are affine along the segment. Fading behind each layer, its depth raster keeps the three nearest sheet layers at each pixel center (two triangles within 10⁻⁷ in depth, the two sides of one edge, are one layer), and each line sample counts the layers among the nine pixels' candidates that contain it and hide it, up to three; runs change where the count does, and each count's runs go to groups named `hidden-` and their layer, then `-2` or `-3`, drawn at the drawing's opacity for that count. That raster takes 24 bytes a pixel instead of 8, about 73 MB at 2000 × 1520 and 290 MB at 4000 × 3040, only while lines fade this way.

**Checks.** Unit tests verify the opacity's range and refusals, arc length continuing along joined segments and restarting at gaps and near-joins, faint hidden runs exactly complementing the visible runs of the existing test, dashes of 8.36 px (55% of 15.2 px) on a 2000 × 1520 page at zoom 1 and 2, dashes shortened by cos θ on a tilted line, and the export record. For dashes on the page they verify the phase against page distances at every vertex (continuing, restarting at gaps, a tilted segment counting only its length across the page), the hairline identity above at 200 points of segments with one end behind the eye, a restart at the near plane, line drawings equal to the dashes in space face on at zoom 1 and 2, every whole dash 8.36 px on a tilted line and on a line receding behind a floor through a lens (where dashes in space vary more than twofold) with starts one 15.2 px period apart, and the record. Browser tests verify, through the real engine and renderer:

- on a sphere seen from +z, see-through pixels against the rule, with the near and far halves' colors taken from opaque drawings of each half alone (the other cut away), for n = 2 and, with the near half cut, n = 1. Away from the chart's poles every channel is within 2 of the rule (within 0.6 measured);
- on the same far half alone, its outline seen through against the opaque drawing's: each of the outline's partly covered opaque pixels gives a share of the sheet's shade, and the see-through pixel must show the same share times α. On a 2000 × 1520 page, 86% of 743 such pixels are partly covered seen through and the shares differ by 0.12 on average; with one sample a pixel, none were, and they differed by 0.35. On a 3000 × 2280 page, past 2²⁴ texels, there is one sample a pixel and the inside still meets the rule within 2. Unit tests check where the sample count changes, at twice the side and at 2²⁴ texels;
- on the trefoil's tube, every pixel of the knot inside the tube equal to `0.65·A + 0.35·L` for faint lines (99% of 1499 pixels within 3 levels), dashed pixels either `0.2·A + 0.8·L` or the tube unchanged (54% and 45%), and every other pixel unchanged. With see-through sheets the knot is still hidden behind the nearest layer, and faint is `0.65·T + 0.35·L` over the see-through drawing `T`;
- the visible-only line drawing's hidden groups and metadata, the panel's controls, links, and an opaque fallback when half-float targets are unavailable;
- spheres of radius 1, 2 and 2.6, one level set, seen down −z and cut at z = −1.85, −1.15, −0.45 and 0.25: at each section pixel fully in the line's ink (for strokes, with its four neighbors, since a stroke's edge blends at its coverage), away from outlines and from other sheets' depths, the number n of sheets above the circle's plane at its (x, y) is computed from the spheres themselves, and the line's opacity over the sheets, `(P − A)·(L − A)/|L − A|²`, must be 0.35·0.5^(min(n, 3) − 1) within 0.03. Behind one, two, three and four layers, 558 of 558, 346 of 348, 716 of 716 and 238 of 238 hairline pixels did, opaque; seen through at α = 0.3, 344 of 348 behind two and all of the rest; and with regular strokes all 7,641. Fading once, 99.6% of them stay at 0.35. A 1 × still of the nested tori equals the live canvas pixel for pixel after the camera is moved, in both themes. Unit tests stack four squares behind one another over a line and check its hidden runs behind one, two and three (the fourth merged with the third) to within a pixel of the squares' edges, also along the squares' diagonals, flat and tilted, where every sample lies on two triangles of each square; a line lying on the second square counted behind the first only; each group's opacity in the file; and that fading once leaves the line drawing as it was;
- a core inside a tube, 360 units from the origin, coming from behind the eye of the wide lens and receding to its vanishing point in segments longest near the eye, measured column by column in a still against the same drawing with hidden lines hidden: on the page, every dash of 57 is 8–9 px (8.36 px) and starts one period (15.2 px) after the last, for hairlines and regular strokes, and the visible-only line drawing's dashes start within 2 px of the drawing's; in space, the same line's dashes run from 1 to 74 px. The 1 × still equals the live canvas pixel for pixel with dashes on the page, after the camera is moved;
- existing studies' visible-only line drawings byte-identical to those recorded before this setting existed, and their PNGs byte-identical after the setting is changed and returned to plain. PNG bytes depend on the rasterizer, so they are compared within a run; against `main` they matched on macOS.

## Spatial line weights

Like seeing through, the weight is a drawing setting, not part of the study. **Hairline** draws every line as WebGL lines, one device pixel wide at any size, as every 3D drawing was before weights; links made before weights open with it. **Fine**, **Regular** (the default) and **Bold** draw strokes whose width is a share of the page, as the 2D notebook's strokes are:

  `w = weight(layer) · m · u`, with `u = min(W/1000, H/760)` device pixels per page pixel,

for a drawing W × H device pixels and m = 0.6, 1 or 1.6. The regular weights in page pixels are 2.2 for the curve, 1.5 for families (filaments, strands, meridians, trajectories, derived curves), 1.4 for a ribbon's edges, a partner thread, the seam and a lamp, 1.8 for section curves, 1.1 for focal and caustic lines, 0.9 for construction lines and outgoing rays, 0.8 for receding strings, connectors and incident rays, and 1.6 for the probe and the cut's edge. So a regular curve is 2.2 px wide on a 1000 × 760 page and 4.4 px in a 2000 × 1520 still, and a live drawing, a still and a video agree in proportion at any size, where a hairline grows relatively thinner as the image grows.

**Strokes.** Each segment of a line batch is one instance of a quad around its two projected ends, made in the vertex stage. The ends are first clipped to the near plane in clip space (z ≥ −w), with the cut's side and the dash length interpolated at the same parameter, so a line passing behind a perspective eye keeps exactly its visible part. Where a segment continues its polyline (its end is exactly the next segment's start, as dashes join them) and the polyline turns by less than 120° on the page, that end is a mitred joint: both quads put their corners on the bisector of the two segments' normals, w/2 + 1 px from either centerline, so neighboring quads share an edge and the stroke neither overlaps itself nor parts at a joint. Where it turns more sharply, as at a cusp, the joint is round: both quads reach w/2 + 1 px past it, each segment covers the pixel as a round cap would, and a pixel there is drawn only by whichever of the two segments is nearer it (the earlier one on a tie), so their union is the round-joined stroke, drawn once. Each segment knows its neighbor at the joint whole, so this holds however unequal their lengths. Only the two segments meeting there are compared: where a polyline folds back along itself, its arms beyond them overlap as any line drawn twice does. A polyline's own ends and ends clipped at the near plane are capped instead: round, reaching w/2 + 1 px past the end, or butt. The fragment stage receives its page position across and along the segment (multiplied by clip w, with w itself, so dividing undoes perspective-correct interpolation and the distances are linear on the page) and covers the pixel by

  `c = clamp(w/2 + ½ − d, 0, 1)`,

with d the distance to the centerline, or to the end of a round cap; a butt end fades over one pixel. This is a one-pixel linear ramp at the stroke's edge. A stroke narrower than a pixel is drawn one pixel wide with c scaled by w, so its total coverage across it is still w. Strokes two pixels wide or more turn c into the share of the pixel's samples written (alpha to coverage) when the canvas is multisampled, so crossing strokes never darken each other's edges and depth is written only where a stroke covers; without samples, they blend. Thinner strokes always blend, with butt ends, because sample coverage counts whole samples (a quarter at a time with four), which lost half of a sub-pixel stroke; they write no depth, since a blended fringe that wrote depth would cut every line drawn behind it later (a 2,000-line veil lost 15% of its filaments' ink where strings crossed them). So strokes are drawn after every sheet, the wide ones first: a thin stroke is hidden by sheets and wide strokes in front of it, and hides nothing itself. Lines behind sheets (**Faint**, **Dashed**) blend at their opacity times c, with butt ends.

**Cuts at round joints.** A retained segment yields only to a retained part of its neighbor. Both evaluate the cut on their centerlines, with each cap belonging to its endpoint. For a cut beyond every plane, the removed interval is the intersection of the planes' positive sides; beyond any plane, the retained interval is the intersection of their nonpositive sides. A crossing at world parameter t becomes page parameter `t w_b / ((1 − t) w_a + t w_b)`, with w_a and w_b the ends' clip w. This keeps a visible arm whole when its neighbor is removed, including partial segments and perspective views. Other joints and hairlines retain their existing cut interpolation. Browser tests reverse a fold and a V without changing their retained geometry, comparing live drawings and independently decoded PNGs for fine and bold strokes, both themes, partial cuts, six planes, both cut rules and tapered perspective strokes.

**Sheets under strokes.** A stroke lies at its centerline's depth across its whole width, but a sheet it lies on is sloped beneath it: at a page distance δ from the centerline the sheet's depth differs by up to `δ · max(|∂z/∂x|, |∂z/∂y|)`. Sheets are therefore drawn with polygon offset factor `1 + max(½, w_max/2)`, w_max the widest stroke drawn, and units 1, so a stroke on a sheet is not cut along one side. Hairlines keep factor 1. The visible-only line drawing samples hiding with the same factor. The cost is at a sheet's outline, where its slope is steep: a stroke just behind the outline can show by up to its half width.

**Checks.** Unit tests verify the page unit, each weight's scale and the layers' order. In the browser, a stroke's width is measured as the sum of its coverage across a circle seen face on, on the live canvas and in a 2000 × 1520 still: 4.24 px for a regular curve against 4.4, 7.24 for bold against 7.04, and every weight live within 10% (or 0.35 px) of its width at the canvas's size, with a hairline within 0.6–1.4 px in both. A bold still of the trefoil's tube is compared pixel by pixel with the visible-only line drawing at the same size and the drawing without lines: of 73,818 changed pixels, 1.1% lie neither over a sheet within reach of a visible line nor over the background within reach of any line (all along outlines), and 99.6% of points sampled along visible lines are inked. With a wide perspective lens close in, where lines pass behind the eye, the figures are 1.1% and 100%. The back halves of the contact circles alone would be about a third of the ink. Faint strokes behind the tube never pass 35% of the way to the knot's color except at the knot's three crossings on the page. A fine stroke 0.47 px wide on a phone-sized canvas sums to 0.47 px of coverage; a regular stroke through a 50° lens keeps its 4.4 px within 4%; a helix coiled around a perspective eye inks nothing outside the reach of its clipped vector lines; strokes lying on a plane seen at a steep angle keep 99% of the ink they have over the background; and in the veil example, drawn dark, no well-inked pixel of a filament returns to the background when its 240 strings are shown. Sharp turns are checked against the round-joined stroke itself: around the point of a V seen face on, turning by 152° and 170°, each pixel's coverage is compared with `clamp(w/2 + ½ − d, 0, 1)`, d the distance to the nearer of the vector export's segments at the same size. A fine stroke 1.32 px wide, which blends, is within 0.024 of it at every pixel and 0.2% in total (capped, it was 0.39 and 0.44 off, parted at the outside of the turn), and the cap past a line folded back on itself within 0.003 (capped, 0.40 off). A bold stroke, drawn by sample coverage, is within 0.19, under one of four samples' share, and 0.2% in total. An MP4's last frame matches the still it ends on (mean difference 1.1 levels), drawn with the weight the export began with. Links, presets, metadata and per-path SVG widths are checked too.

### Strokes that taper with depth

**Lines → Depth → Taper with distance** draws strokes, seen through a perspective lens, as a line of one thickness in space would look. Like the weight, it is a drawing setting, not part of the study; **Even**, every stroke its weight's width wherever it is, is the default, and links made before it have no depth and open even. A tapered stroke at a point of clip w is

  `w_taper = w · clamp(F/w_clip, ¼, 4)`,

with w its weight's width above and F the camera's *focus*, the clip w of the plane where strokes keep their weight's width (`scene.ts`'s `Camera.focus`). Clip w is a point's distance ahead of the eye over the radius, so the factor is the focus plane's distance over the point's: twice as wide at half the distance, half as wide at twice it. For the turntable's lenses the focus plane is the plane through the view's target, the one a lens draws at the orthographic scale, so a stroke on it is exactly as wide as through the orthographic camera; it moves with the target as zoom moves the eye. Riding a ray, whose lens has no target, it is one study radius ahead of the eye. The factor is held within [¼, 4] so that a line passing beside the eye does not fill the page and one far away keeps some ink. An orthographic camera has w_clip = 1 = F at every point, and hairlines are one pixel at any depth, so neither changes.

**Drawing.** Each end of a segment, after clipping at the near plane, takes the width of its own clip w, and its quad reaches w_taper/2 + 1 px from the centerline at that end, so the quad is a trapezoid. A mitred joint lies at one point with one w, so neighboring quads still share an edge. The fragment stage receives both ends' widths and interpolates them linearly along the segment on the page by its exact page position along the segment, so the width at a point is the ends' interpolation: 1/w is affine on the page, so between ends inside the range this is the factor itself, and a segment whose ends lie on either side of a limit is interpolated between their held widths. Coverage, the sub-pixel fade and round or butt ends then use that local width exactly as above. A segment is drawn as a wide stroke (sample coverage, round ends, writing depth) when its ends' mean width is two pixels or more, and as a thin one otherwise, so a tapered batch is drawn in two passes, its wide segments with every other wide stroke before its thin ones. Sheets are pushed back by the widest tapered stroke the study's bounds allow: the factor at the bounds' nearest point, a radius nearer than their center, or 4 when that point reaches the eye.

**Line drawing (SVG).** An SVG path has one stroke width, so the line drawing draws a tapered line in steps. A run between two page points is split where its factor `F/w_clip`, affine on the page, crosses a limit or halfway, in ratio, between powers of 1.05; each step is drawn at the weight's width times its power of 1.05, or a limit, in a path of that width, with round ends. So every point of the line drawing is within a factor √1.05 of the drawing's width, and steps of one width continue one another's paths. Faint and dashed hidden lines taper the same way; where two steps of a faint line meet, their round ends overlap by a fraction of a pixel.

**Records.** Exports record `strokes.depth: "taper"` and its rule only when strokes taper, so other files are unchanged. The two examples **A braided cable running to the horizon** and **A coiled trefoil, near and far** open with bold tapered strokes through the wide and the normal lens.

**Checks.** Unit tests observe the factor through the drawing's own camera: d/D at five distances to 10⁻⁹ for two zooms and radii, the limits at a tenth and ten times d, and 1 for even strokes, older links and the orthographic camera. The line drawing of a line receding from within a quarter of d to beyond four times it has every vertex within √1.05 (plus 0.2%) of the weight times the factor read from the vertex's own page position, steps that are powers of 1.05 or limits, both limits reached, its paths joined end to end, and the visible-only drawing equal to it with nothing to hide; even, orthographic and hairline drawings are unchanged. The sheets' offset is checked against the bounds' nearest point. In the browser, a circle turned 0.6 rad through the normal lens is crossed by the page's middle row at distances d ∓ 2 sin 0.6: in a 2000 × 1520 still its regular stroke measures 5.24 px on the near side against 5.17 and 3.74 px on the far side against 3.83, the live drawing keeps their ratio within 10%, the line drawing's widths span the same range, and even strokes measure 4.4 px on both sides within 8%. A line receding along the view's axis, panned aside, is measured column by column in a still: down each of 93 columns the stroke's coverage is within max(0.35 px, 6%) of the weight times d/D, and of four times the weight in the 23 columns nearer than a quarter of d. Every existing example's thumbnail redraws byte for byte.

## Spatial camera paths

**Fly through key views** animates the camera alone; the study is fixed. A path is 2–12 key views, each the drawing's manual camera: yaw ψ, pitch φ ∈ [−1.5, 1.5], zoom z from 0.2 to the drawing's own limit, 8, or 1,000 in a study [refined to its view](#refinement-to-the-view), and pan (p_x, p_y), all about the study's own fitted center C and radius r, with a name and, after the first, a whole number of added turns m ∈ [−8, 8]. The camera is a turntable. Its rotation R(ψ, φ) turns about the world's y axis by ψ, then tilts by φ, and never rolls, and the pitch stops short of ±π/2. So (ψ, φ) names every orientation the camera can take, without a singularity, and orientations are interpolated as these two angles. Quaternions are not used: there is no roll to carry, and so no sign of q against −q to choose.

**Timing.** Each view after the first carries a leg time hₖ ∈ [0.1, 10], the time of the leg that arrives at it relative to the others, 1 unless set; the first view has none. View k is reached at progress tₖ = (h₁ + … + hₖ)/(h₁ + … + hₙ₋₁), so each leg takes the share of the duration in proportion to its time, and with every time equal (the default) view k is reached at k/(n − 1). Progress is the motion's own, after repeat and pace (see [seamless loops](#seamless-loops)): back and forth and easing spend the duration as before, with each leg its share of the motion. At those instants the camera *is* that key view, field for field, so the drawing is exactly the one its manual camera draws; a progress whose leg time lies within 4ε of the total leg time from that view's is that view, so rounding the sum cannot leave the camera a hair short of it. Moving a view keeps its turns and leg time with it, so the legs arriving at each view are unchanged; the view that becomes the first, by moving or removal, drops them.

**Turning.** Yaw is unwrapped along the path: Ψ₀ = ψ₀ and Ψₖ = Ψₖ₋₁ + w(ψₖ − Ψₖ₋₁) + 2πmₖ, where w(d) = d − 2π·round(d/2π) ∈ [−π, π) is the shorter way round. Exactly half a turn goes the negative way; one added turn takes the positive way. Positive turns increase yaw, as the orbit animation and dragging right do. Two equal views with m = 1 and no pan reproduce the orbit animation.

**Styles.** **Steady** interpolates Ψ, φ and ln z linearly on each leg: the camera turns and tilts at constant rates, and zooms by equal factors in equal times. A leg's time changes how long it takes, not how it moves: at the same fraction of the leg, the camera is the same. **Smooth** interpolates the same three by monotone piecewise cubic Hermite interpolation through all the views at their times (Fritsch and Carlson's method with the end conditions of Moler's `pchip`, both for unequal spacing). With δₖ the rate of leg k, Δyₖ/hₖ, an interior view's slope is 0 where the two legs' rates differ in sign or one is 0, and otherwise the weighted harmonic mean `(w₁ + w₂)/(w₁/δₖ₋₁ + w₂/δₖ)` with `w₁ = 2hₖ + hₖ₋₁` and `w₂ = hₖ + 2hₖ₋₁`, which leans toward the shorter leg's rate; an end's slope is `((2h₀ + h₁)δ₀ − h₀δ₁)/(h₀ + h₁)`, set to 0 if its sign differs from δ₀ and limited to 3δ₀ beside a reversal. With equal times these are the plain harmonic mean and `(3δ₀ − δ₁)/2`, computed so that they round exactly as before, and a path without leg times flies bit for bit as it did. It passes through every view with a rate continuous in time, and on each leg stays between the leg's two values, so it never overshoots a view. It rests (rate 0) at a view that is a turning point of that quantity, or next to a leg that does not change it. With two views both styles are linear.

**Easing at key views.** The optional **Ease key-view transitions** setting replaces each leg's time fraction f with `q(f) = 6f⁵ − 15f⁴ + 10f³` before evaluating the entire camera, including its framed point and any geometry pivot. This preserves the selected style's route, key views, added turns and leg times. Since q′ and q″ vanish at both ends, the camera's velocity and acceleration tend to zero on both sides of every key view, including an open flight's endpoints and a loop's seam. A pan can therefore settle before a stationary zoom begins. This applies to two-view flights and both path styles in both notebooks, in playback, scrubbing and exports. With easing enabled, **Smooth** also rests at every view. The overall **Pace** and repeat mapping still runs first. The setting defaults off; absent in older studies and links, camera output is unchanged.

**Framing.** The camera turns about its *framed point*: the point in the middle of the page on the plane through C that faces the camera, `T = C − Rᵀ(p_x, p_y, 0)`. Between views k and k + 1 the framed point moves along the segment from Tₖ to Tₖ₊₁ by `σ = (1/z − 1/zₖ)/(1/zₖ₊₁ − 1/zₖ)`, computed as `expm1(−(ln z − ln zₖ))/expm1(−(ln zₖ₊₁ − ln zₖ))`, or by the leg's time fraction when the leg keeps its zoom. With the orientation held, this is the one interpolation of zoom and pan in which every point of the page moves on a straight line through a fixed point of the page: the place framed at view k + 1 comes straight to the middle as the camera zooms in, instead of first sweeping outward as a linear pan would. When the orientation also changes, the rule still keeps the framed point in the middle and moves it as stated, but no point need stay still on the page. The camera is then drawn as a manual camera about C with pan `(R(C − T))ₓ,ᵧ`, so depth keeps the study's range. A point on the plane through C facing a view, panned to the middle, is that view's framed point, and a turn added to a view repeated keeps it in the middle all the way round.

**Turn about** chooses the framed points. **Center plane** (the default, and every link made before the setting) takes them on the plane through C, as above, so a turn holds a detail off that plane only approximately. **Geometry in the middle** moves each view's framed point along its line of sight, `Tₖ = C − Rₖᵀ(p_x, p_y, 0) + sₖ b̂ₖ`, to the drawn geometry nearest the viewer there: the largest sₖ among the points where the line through the middle of the page crosses a shaded sheet, and the points of lines nearest that line (across it) that the view draws within 5% of half the page's shorter side of the middle. Only geometry the view draws counts: the layers shown, inside its clip volume (so through a lens, nothing behind the eye), and not hidden by the cut. A view with nothing there keeps sₖ = 0. The depths are found once, when playback starts, from the study as then drawn, through the projection the path flies in; the legs then move the framed point as above. Under **Smooth**, with three or more views and some depth not 0, the framed point also keeps its rate through each view, as the camera's angles and zoom do. Alone, leg k − 1 reaches view k at the velocity `Lₖ = (Tₖ − Tₖ₋₁)σ̇` and leg k leaves it at `Rₖ = (Tₖ₊₁ − Tₖ)σ̇`, with σ̇ the rate of each leg's σ in time at that view under the smooth zoom (1/h on a leg that keeps its zoom). The view's velocity is `Vₖ = (hₖLₖ + hₖ₋₁Rₖ)/(hₖ₋₁ + hₖ)`, each leg's weighed by the other's time, as a parabola's slope through three points is. An open path's first and last views keep their one leg's velocity, and a loop's seam (`cyclic`) is a view between the last leg and the first. Leg k, at fraction f, then adds the cubic Hermite terms `hₖ[(f³ − 2f² + f)(Vₖ − Rₖ) + (f³ − f²)(Vₖ₊₁ − Lₖ₊₁)]`, which leave its ends in place and set its velocity there to the views'. So the framed point bends through a view instead of turning a corner there, and rests where both legs rest. A leg whose velocities already agree with its views' moves as before, and the terms can carry the framed point off the segment between its leg's two framed points by at most 4hₖ/27 times the change they make at each end. Steady, paths of two views, the center plane and depths that are all 0 move the framed point exactly as above. Every point on a view's line of sight is drawn in the middle of the page in either projection, so each view is still drawn exactly as its manual camera draws it, and only the legs between views change. A detail in the middle of a view repeated with an added turn stays in the middle all the way round, within the single precision the geometry is drawn in. Line segments are first restricted to the parts the cut and clip volume keep, then to the 5% disk on the page, before their closest point is chosen. If every point is equally close across the line of sight (a segment parallel to it), the visible point nearest the eye wins; reversing the vertices does not change it.

**Geometry pivots through a lens.** The distance ahead of a key view's eye to its framed point is positive, `Dₖ = dₖ − sₖ`, with `dₖ = 1.16r/(zₖ tan(α/2))`. Between keys the path interpolates ln D in its chosen style, so D stays positive and between its two endpoint distances, and places the eye at `E = T + D b̂`. A repeated view with an added turn keeps this eye distance constant, so the eye turns around the framed geometry instead of crossing it as the center plane turns. The camera still clips about the original study's center and radius: far reaches four radii past the center; near is the greater of the distance four radii short of it and `min(d/100, D)`. The original zoom's distance d remains the reference for strokes that taper with depth, so strokes meet each key view continuously too. Each key view itself is unchanged, including its eye and clipping; a path whose depths are all zero retains the center-plane camera bit for bit. A captured intermediate view converts from its actual eye into the study's manual camera, within the existing orbit and zoom limits.

A view taken from a drawing framed about other bounds (C′, r′), such as a finished animation's camera, is converted to the study's bounds so that every point is drawn where it was: `z = z′·r/r′` and `p = p′ + (R(C − C′))ₓ,ᵧ`. Only the depth range changes, to the study's.

**While the geometry moves.** The same path can be the **Animation camera** of a reveal, a parameter animation, a trace of light, the probe's motion or a peel; the orbit turns the camera itself and offers none. The camera at progress p is the path's camera at p, as above, about the bounds (C, r) of the study as drawn when playback starts, which its views were taken about. It never refits to a frame's own bounds, as **Fit each frame** does, so it depends on time alone. Each frame is drawn with the camera of its own time: a parameter frame that the engine delivers late is shown with the camera of the time it was computed for, never with the camera of the moment it arrives. Geometry that grows beyond the starting study can leave the page, and points more than four radii r from C along the view are clipped, as with **Hold current view**.

**Refinement in pieces.** In a study refined to its view, frames zoomed in past the fitted framing (z > 1) are drawn refined to what they show, as the still is ([refinement to the view](#refinement-to-the-view)), and key views zoom to 1,000×. As in the [2D flight](#planar-camera-paths), each frame is drawn from a refinement made once per *piece* of the flight (`flightPieces` in `web/spatial/path.ts`), but a turning camera shows no single rectangle, so a piece is a few of its frames' own views, sent together (`views`, `engine3.Request.Views`, at most 32). Go refines a piece of curve that meets the margin of any of them to the finest tolerance and chord limit those it meets ask, each at its own depth: the views' tolerances are the same multiple of their half diagonals, so the focus scales the first view's by each view's half diagonal over the first's, times its depth w (`viewsFocus` in `engine3/view.go`). One view is refined exactly as alone. Progress is sampled 512 times per leg. A frame is kept as a view of its piece once it has moved from the last kept by a step: its zoom by more than 5%, or, by more than a quarter of the narrower frame's half shorter side h (`1.16r/z`, or, about the geometry through a lens, `D tan(α/2)`), its framed point across the line of sight plus, as the line of sight turns by θ, `θ(|T − C| + r)`, how far geometry anywhere in the study moves across the page. So every frame between two kept views lies within both's margins, at every depth an orthographic view draws, and is refined at least within 5% as finely as it needs. A piece closes when it would hold 16 views, or its zoom would span more than 2×, and keeps the frame that closes it. Pieces are refined when first needed, with the two after and the one before; while playback has a second engine, pieces alternate between the two. They are kept for the session, so playback, scrubbing, repeats and export draw the same curves. Frames at the fitted zoom or wider draw the study refined to no view. Flown as the **Animation camera**, a reveal, a trace, the probe's motion and a peel cut their frames from the same pieces; parameter frames are calculated anyway, so each, its ends included, is refined to its own view.

**Checks.** Unit tests observe the path through the drawing's own projection (`scene.ts`), not a second copy of the formulas: each view reproduced exactly at its time in both styles; the unpanned full turn equal to the orbit animation within 10⁻⁹ px; the shorter way across ±π, ±1 added turns, and the half-turn tie; a yaw that increases monotonically through two turns; continuity (no point moves more than 25 px in 1/3000 of a path that turns 2½ times and zooms 6×); smooth's equal one-sided rates at an interior view (within 10⁻³) where steady's differ, a rest at a peak, and no pitch or zoom outside each leg's two views; an 8× zoom whose framed point moves monotonically and on a straight line to the middle; a framed point kept in the middle through a full turn; with **Geometry in the middle**, a short line well in front of the plane kept within 0.01 px of the middle through a full turn in both styles, orthographic, through the normal lens and at 20°, where the plane pivot lets it wander over 200 px, visible inside the clip volume at every step and drawn by the line renderer, and refused behind the eye of a 120° lens; the framed depth of a line across the middle, of the nearer of two lines, of a line tilted in depth where it passes the middle, of a line just inside and just outside reach, of a sheet at its crossing whichever corner comes first and of none beside it, of geometry inside and beyond the far clip, and with the cut hiding the nearer line (and not, when it reaches only sheets); cut-line remnants, both multi-plane cut rules, near/far clip remnants, sight-parallel lines in either order and their reach boundary; positive eye distances in both styles, exact key cameras and continuous projection, clip depth and taper reference at keys, unchanged zero-depth perspective paths and captured intermediate cameras; a level sheet's framed point on the sheet and in the middle of the page; depths of 0 flying exactly as the plane does; and every view unchanged whatever its depth; under **Smooth** about the geometry, the drawing's rate on the page (and through a lens the framed point's own) equal either side of every view and a loop's seam within 10⁻⁶ relative, with equal and unequal legs, orthographically and through the normal lens and at 20°, where the center plane turns a corner of over 10⁻²; a path flown backward as its views reversed, open and looped, within 10⁻⁶ px, and a leg that keeps its zoom passing its views at one rate; a small detail framed by three views from different sides kept within 2 px of the middle, where the plane lets it stray over 20 px; and paths about the plane in both styles, open and looped, steady paths and paths of two views about the geometry, and smooth paths whose depths are all 0, held at 1,001 progress values to the implementation before this rule, to six significant figures (every preset's path, in each of these ways, was compared with it field for field when the rule was added); a converted view drawing every point within 10⁻⁹ px; and the refusals. For leg times they check each view reached exactly at its share, including times whose sums are not exact in binary; that steady flies every leg the same at the same fraction of it whatever its time; that times of 1 draw bit for bit as a path without them and equal times of 2.5 within 10⁻⁹ px; smooth's equal one-sided rates in time (within 10⁻⁴ relative) at a view between a leg of 0.5 and one of 3, no pitch or zoom outside a leg's two views, a rest at a peak and continuity; a loop's seam at one rate with legs of 0.4, 2 and 3; and the refusals, a time outside [0.1, 10] and a first view with a time other than 1. Every existing preset's path, steady and smooth, open and looped, was compared at 1,001 progress values against the implementation before leg times, field for field. Browser tests check through the real notebook that keys come from the drawing, that the drawing at a view's time is pixel-identical to the manual camera at that view, Stop and Back to study, links, and the decoded MP4's first and last frames against still PNGs at the first and last views. Browser tests also check **Leg time**: each view's share as the views report it, the camera at a view's time and between, the leg named in the readout, a refused time named by its view, a copied link, and removing the first view or setting a view to the drawing. On **A rhumb line spiraling into its pole**, legs of 1, 6 and 0.8 are named at their times, and on the long leg the camera looks straight down the z axis at the zoom 8^((p − t₁)/(t₂ − t₁)) to 10⁻⁹, with the pole within a pixel of the middle of the page. On **Viviani's curve, from every side**, the line drawing (SVG) at the end-on view has every vertex of the curve within 0.05 px of one circle, and at the side-on view every vertex within 2·10⁻³ (in squared world units) of `y² = z²(1 − (z/2a)²)`. Halfway around the crossing, the curve passes within 3 px of the middle of the page. With Viviani's curve alone and a view framing its top (0, 0, 2a), 2a in front of the plane, and the same view again a turn on, **Geometry in the middle** keeps the curve within a pixel of the middle at every eighth of the turn, and **Center plane** lets it stray more than 20 px; a link carries the setting, and one without it turns about the plane. Through the normal lens close to its top, a full turn keeps the curve visible in the middle during playback; a six-frame MP4 decodes with 400 ms duration, its first and last frames matching the first key view and its frame at progress 0.8 matching the paused drawing there. A flight's pieces are checked through the drawing's own `refinementView` against the engine's documented rule, at 4,001 progress values on five flights (orthographic into a detail at 800×, turning at 200×, and panning at 50×, a smooth perspective there and back, and about geometry off the center plane through a wide lens): every point a zoomed frame draws, across its page and, in an orthographic view, anywhere in the study's depth, lies in the margin of one of its piece's views whose tolerance there is within 1.1 of the frame's own; each piece spans at most 2.05× of zoom. Native tests (`engine3/views_test.go`) refine a trefoil to two windows of different sizes and directions, each window's chords within 1.5 of its own tolerances and the wide one's not refined to the narrow one's, the same in either order; a helix seen from two eyes 12 units apart, each chord within 1.5 tolerances at its depth from either eye where the near view alone strays twice the far one's; a piece of one view byte-identical to the view; hand-placed boxes met only when grown, scaled alike; refusals (a view and views together, more than 32, a view that is not a frame); and **Wheels within wheels** drawn unbroken, its slowest point still moving. In the browser, that flight is paused at 76%, 82% and its end (79×), alone, over a reveal and over parameters, and its exported line drawing's chords lie within 1.5 of the frame's own view tolerance of the exact curve, its strokes ending only at the page's edges; without refinement to the view the flight is refused at its first view past 8×.

### Riding a ray

While light is traced (see [Spatial mirrors](#spatial-mirrors-reflected-rays-and-caustics) and the trace's optical path), **Animation camera → Ride a ray · perspective** replaces the orthographic turntable with a perspective camera that follows one representative ray.

**The perspective camera.** A lens is an eye E, a forward direction f, an up direction u made perpendicular to f, a field of view α across the page's shorter side, and near and far distances n < f_far along f. With right r = f × u, a point P at distance D = (P − E)·f ahead is drawn at `((P − E)·r, (P − E)·u) / (D tan(α/2))` half page sides from the middle, and its window depth is `(1/n − 1/D)/(1/n − 1/f_far)`, affine in 1/D, from 0 at the near plane to 1 at the far one. Points nearer than n, behind the eye or beyond f_far are not drawn. In clip coordinates, w = D/ρ with ρ the framing radius, and orthographic views keep w = 1 exactly, so nothing they draw changes. Lines and triangles are clipped in homogeneous coordinates before the divide by w, so a segment or sheet passing behind the eye is cut at the near plane rather than drawn inverted. Quantities interpolated across a primitive (the cut's side, a dash's arc length) are interpolated perspective-correctly, as a/w and 1/w, in the drawing and in the line drawing (SVG) alike. Lighting is unchanged: it depends only on the normal in camera coordinates.

**The ray.** The ray is the representative ray drawn at sample (i, j), a crossing of the parameter curves, chosen as **Ray at u** and **Ray at v**; a crossing the light does not reach has none and is refused. Its polyline is the trace's own: from S, where its light is at τ = 0, along the unit incident direction I with index n₁, reaching the surface point X at τ_X; then along the unit outgoing direction D = (end − X)/|end − X| of the drawn ray with index n_out, which is n₂ for refraction and n₁ for reflection and for total internal reflection, until τ_end = τ_X + n_out ℓ at the drawn end. Which of reflected, refracted or totally reflected it is comes from the study, never from the direction. Before S the polyline continues straight back along −I. The virtual extension behind the surface is never part of it.

**The ride.** With follow distance d and window Δ, both optical path given in framing radii ρ, the head at optical path τ is the point of the polyline at τ_h = min(τ, τ_end), exactly where the traced ray's light has reached, and the eye is the point at τ_e = τ_h − d. Once the head reaches the drawn end the camera rests. The camera looks along the incident ray until the eye is Δ/2 from the surface, and along the outgoing ray once it is Δ/2 past it. Between, with s = (τ_e − τ_X + Δ/2)/Δ, it turns by the smoothstep `σ = 3s² − 2s³`, which starts and ends at rest:

`f = cos(σθ) I + sin(σθ) u₀,  u = −sin(σθ) I + cos(σθ) u₀,  θ = atan2(|D⊥|, D·I),`

where D⊥ = D − (D·I)I and u₀ = D⊥/|D⊥|. So up starts toward the side the ray turns to, the whole turn is about the fixed axis I × u₀, and the camera never rolls: r = f × u is constant through the ride. No quaternion sign arises, since the rotation is about one axis by an angle in [0, π]. Where the light sets no side, D ∥ ±I (straight through, or straight back from a mirror at normal incidence), u₀ is +z made perpendicular to I or, where I is vertical, +y; a reversal then turns over that up. While eye and head share a segment, outside the window, the head is on the axis, in the middle of the page, a distance d/n of length ahead. The field of view is 60°; the near plane is `min(ρ/20, d/(4 max(n₁, n_out)))`, a quarter of the shortest length the follow distance can be; the far plane is |E − C| + 4ρ, four radii past the study's center as the orthographic camera's depth. The ride is a function of progress alone, about the study's bounds when playback starts, so pause, scrubbing and export draw each frame with the camera of its time.

A finished ride releases its camera; orbiting, panning or zooming it leaves the ray for the view held when playback started, in that view's own projection (see [the manual camera in perspective](#the-manual-camera-in-perspective)), and Home returns to the ray.

**Checks.** Unit tests observe the ride through the drawing's projection and the trace's own geometry. The perspective camera draws a pinhole's picture to 10⁻⁶ px, with depth affine in 1/D, a portrait page measured across its width, an up leaning along forward made perpendicular, and points behind the eye or outside the near and far planes refused. The line drawing cuts a segment at the near plane where it crosses it; a floor reaching behind the eye hides a line below it entirely and one above it not at all; a cut floor hides a line exactly up to where its cut is seen (within 1 px of x = 6 for a cut at x = 5 seen from height 1 over a line at depth 0.2); and dashes behind it keep their length in space within 0.05 px, including runs that begin or end partway along a segment. On a paraboloid under axial light the traced ray's head is drawn in the middle of the page within 10⁻⁶ px wherever eye and head share a segment, a follow distance ahead; the camera looks exactly along I before the window and D after it, turns θ/2 at the eye's arrival and 0.028θ a tenth of the way in, turns monotonically and continuously (no step of τ turns it more than 1.5θΔτ/Δ), and never rolls. The eye stands where the polyline puts it, upstream of the wavefront, past the surface at index n₂, and under total reflection at n₁, rests at the drawn end, never leaves the real outgoing ray for a virtual one, turns over +y when light returns straight back, and turns 0.2° in all for a grazing ray at 89.9°. Browser tests check the controls and refusals, the head in the middle of the page in the line drawing of the real notebook at 0.1 and 1, the shaded still inked within a pixel of over 90% of the points along the line drawing's visible lines (in Chromium and WebKit), pause, release, orbit and Home, Back to study, links, and the decoded MP4's first and last frames against stills of the same frames. The ridden ray itself is seen end-on, as a point.

## The manual camera in perspective

**Projection** above the 3D drawing chooses how the manual camera projects: **Orthographic** (the default), or a perspective lens of field of view α across the page's shorter side, **narrow** (30°), **normal** (50°) or **wide** (90°), or **chosen angle**, any whole number of degrees from 1° to 150° set with **Lens angle** beside the named views under the drawing. The camera is still the turntable of [camera paths](#spatial-camera-paths), with yaw ψ, pitch φ, zoom z and pan (p_x, p_y) about the study's center C and radius r. Its rows are right `r̂ = (cos ψ, 0, sin ψ)`, up `û = (sin φ sin ψ, cos φ, −sin φ cos ψ)` and back `b̂ = (−cos φ sin ψ, sin φ, cos φ cos ψ)`, the rows of the orthographic rotation R(ψ, φ).

**The lens.** Its target is the framed point `T = C − p_x r̂ − p_y û + s b̂`, with s the target depth (0 until a zoom moves it; see below), and its eye is `E = T + d b̂` with

`d = 1.16 r / (z tan(α/2)),`

looking along −b̂ with up û, through the [perspective camera](#riding-a-ray) of the ride. So the plane through T facing the camera is drawn at the orthographic scale, z/(1.16 r) per half page side: a point on it is drawn where the orthographic camera draws it, switching projection keeps that plane's size, and panning by the drag's pixels moves it exactly as before. Points nearer the eye are drawn larger, in proportion to 1/D. Zoom *dollies*: it moves the eye toward T, so dz is constant and doubling the zoom halves the distance, while the lens's angle never changes. At zoom 8 through the wide lens the eye is 0.145 r from T, inside most studies. Yaw and pitch orbit the eye about T, as they orbit the orthographic view.

**Zooming toward a detail.** The drawing depends only on the eye, the orientation and α, not on d, so T can slide along the line of sight without moving anything. A zoom through a lens about a point of the page (the wheel's pointer, the fingers' midpoint, or the middle of the page for the keys) first finds the geometry drawn there (`pickAt` in `web/spatial/zoom.ts`): where the line of sight through that point crosses a shaded sheet, and the point of each line drawn within 12 px of it on the page that is nearest it there, found along the line in space with perspective-correct interpolation. The one drawn nearest the point wins, and of those drawn within a pixel of that, the nearest the viewer; only what the view draws counts (the layers shown, the probe and the cut's edge, inside the clip volume, and not hidden by the cut). If a point P is found at distance `D = b̂·(E − P) > 0` ahead of the eye, the camera is _anchored_ at it: the zoom becomes `z′ = 1.16 r / (D tan(α/2))`, held within the zoom limits, and the target depth `s′ = s + d − 1.16 r / (z′ tan(α/2))`, so the eye stays put and the plane through T′ passes through P, drawn at the orthographic scale z′. The zoom then multiplies z′ about where P is drawn, with the pan correction of the orthographic zoom, which holds every point of that plane in place. So P stays exactly where it was drawn (a line's point within reach of the pointer as well as a point under it), every step magnifies it by the step's factor, and the eye approaches it geometrically without reaching it: at the 1,000× limit P is drawn at 1,000 times the orthographic scale of zoom 1, the zoom's own meaning, wherever it lies. Over empty page, or without a lens, zoom keeps its target plane as before. A run of wheel steps at one place picks once, until the camera, the pointer or what is drawn changes. The orthographic camera ignores s: switching projection keeps the plane through T′ at its size.

**The chosen angle.** Changing α at a fixed zoom is a *dolly zoom*: d changes with 1/tan(α/2), so the eye slides along the line of sight while the plane through T keeps its size, and everything nearer or farther than that plane grows or shrinks against it. A point at depth h in front of the plane (toward the eye) is drawn larger than on the plane by `d/(d − h)`, so the narrowest angle, 1°, puts the eye `1.16 r / (z tan 0.5°) ≈ 133 r / z` away, where every point of the study is drawn within 1% of its orthographic scale at zoom 1; at 150° the eye is `0.311 r / z` from T, inside the study at any zoom above 0.31. The limits keep d between 664 r (1°, zoom 0.2) and 0.039 r (150°, zoom 8), where the depth rule below still holds the study. Choosing the angle from a named lens starts at that lens's angle, so the drawing does not move; from orthographic, at the angle last chosen (50° at first). A named lens draws exactly as the chosen angle of its own value, and ignores the chosen angle.

**Depth.** With ρ = |E − C|, the far plane is ρ + 4r, four radii past the center as the orthographic depth, and the near plane is `max(ρ − 4r, d/100)`: four radii short of the center, or a hundredth of the eye's distance to its target once the eye is that close. Geometry nearer than that, or behind the eye, is clipped in homogeneous coordinates, so a sheet passing beside the eye is cut rather than drawn inverted.

**The same camera everywhere.** The animation camera takes the projection of the view held when playback starts: Hold current view, the framing modes and the orbit keep it, and a [camera path](#spatial-camera-paths) flies its key views through it (key views record no projection of their own). A ride's lens replaces it while riding, and leaving a released ride returns to the held view, projection included. A [seamless loop](#seamless-loops) needs equal projections at both ends. A view taken as a key view from a drawing framed about other bounds (C′, r′) keeps every point where it was: pan converts as in orthographic, and the eye stays put, so the new distance behind the new target is `d + s − b̂·(C − C′)` and `z = 1.16 r / ((d + s − b̂·(C − C′)) tan(α/2))`, clamped to the zoom range like any key view; key views carry no target depth. Hold current view keeps s in playback and exports; the framing modes, the orbit's framing and camera paths draw about their own center plane. Exports draw through the same camera and record `view.projection` with a statement of the lens, and with the chosen angle its `angle` in degrees; orthographic files are unchanged. Links carry the projection as `projection` and the chosen angle as `lensAngle`, a whole number of degrees from 1 to 150 kept while a named lens is shown; links made before them open orthographic, and at 50° when the chosen angle is then picked. A seamless loop through the chosen angle needs the same angle at both ends, and the same target depth. Links carry s as the view's `targetDepth` only once a zoom has set it; links made before it have none, which is 0. **Reset view** and Home reset orbit, pan, zoom and target depth, keeping the projection.

**Limits.** Dashes behind sheets keep the length in space set from the zoom, so near dashes look longer than far ones. **Lines (SVG) · visible only, sampled** counts toward its work limit (200 million at 2000 × 1520, in proportion to the page's area) only the pixels between where each sheet triangle's edges cross each row of the page, one more on each side, and one for the row. Close to the eye a triangle's corners can lie far off the page, where its bounding box on the page would be much larger than the part it covers; a fan of 400 wedges about the target at zoom 8 through the wide lens costs 1.4 page areas this way and 90 by boxes, and across every preset, orthographic and through the normal and wide lenses at zooms 1, 3 and 8, the costliest view fell from 580 million to 22 million, with every drawing unchanged. Only sheets that really cover the page about 65 times over exceed the limit; **every line** still exports. Lighting depends only on normals, so it does not change with distance.

**Checks.** Unit tests observe the camera through the drawing's own projection: an orthographic projection returns the same camera as no projection at all; for each lens, at three views including the pitch limits, zoom 0.2 and 8 and large pans, five points are drawn where an independent pinhole at the stated eye draws them, within 10⁻⁶ px; points on the plane through T are drawn where the orthographic camera draws them, and a point a radius nearer is not; at four zooms, a point halfway to T along the line of sight is drawn in the middle of the page and one just behind the eye not at all; far away through the narrow lens every point of the study's sphere is inside the clip volume and one 4.5 radii past C is not; with the eye inside the study, a point d/50 ahead is drawn, one d/500 ahead is not, and the far side of the study is drawn; a converted key view draws five points within 10⁻⁶ px of where the view drew them; a camera path flies in the held view's projection; and loops compare projections. For the chosen angle, at 1°, 7.5°, 30°, 50°, 90°, 120° and 150° and the same three views, points are drawn where an independent pinhole of that angle draws them, within 10⁻⁵ px; at the named lenses' angles the camera equals the named lens's, which ignores a chosen angle; the plane through T stays where orthographic draws it at every angle while a nearer point moves farther from it as the angle widens (by under 2 px at 1°); the study's sphere stays in the clip volume at both limits and zooms 0.2, 1 and 8; key views keep every point in place at 7.5° and 140°; a path carries the angle; and loops tell 140° from 141° but not a named lens's ignored angle. Browser tests check the control, a caption that leaves the lens to the control, orbit, keys, drag and Reset view in perspective, the shaded still inked within a pixel of over 90% of the points along the line drawing's visible lines (and under 75% against the orthographic still), export metadata, the projection during an orbit and a camera path and after release, a copied link, and each preset's projection, view and flight. For the chosen angle they check **Lens angle** (its limits, its start from the lens shown before or the angle last chosen, and a redraw at each step), that it moves no control and stays outside the drawing at desktop and phone widths, the still and line drawing agreeing at 120° and 20°, metadata, Reset view, an orbit, a copied link, and the presets that open with one. Every existing thumbnail redraws byte-identical.

Zooming toward a detail is checked in `tests/spatial-zoom-detail.spec.ts`. Through the normal lens, points are drawn where an independent pinhole about `T = C − p_x r̂ − p_y û + s b̂` draws them, within 10⁻⁶ px, at three depths and zooms, and orthographic views ignore s. A detail five units in front of the center plane, which a zoom toward that plane would put behind the eye by zoom 3, is zoomed into for 40 steps of 1.25 to the 1,000× limit: it stays drawn within 10⁻³ px of the pointer and ahead of the eye, each step magnifies it by 1.25 to 10⁻⁵ (the single precision its sheet is drawn in), and at the limit it is drawn at 1,000 times the scale of zoom 1. A line picked 7 px beside the pointer stays within 0.02 px of where it was drawn past 100×, and zooming out backs away from a detail behind the center plane the same way. Anchoring without zooming moves no point by 10⁻⁶ px, including a detail nearer than the zoom limit allows the target, which stops at the limit's plane. With nothing picked, or orthographic, the zoom equals the formula before it exactly. The pick takes the nearer of two sheets under the pointer in either order, a line within reach at its nearest point and not one 1.5 reaches away, a line receding at a slant at the point under the pointer within 10⁻⁵, a sheet's interior but not a point just past its third edge, a far line or sheet under the pointer over a nearer line 5–8 px away, a nearer line within a pixel of a sheet, nothing hidden by the cut or 0.01 to 100 units behind the eye, and the same point within 10⁻³ in geometry measured from a drawing origin 7,000 units out. A view with a target depth taken as a key view about other bounds keeps five points within 10⁻⁶ px; Hold current view keeps s and the other camera modes drop it; loops tell target depths apart. Targeted mutations of the lens, the anchoring, the zoom limits, the pick's cut, origin, reach, perspective-correct interpolation, triangle test and preference, the animation camera, the loop comparison and the key view were each caught; two survivors are equivalent (the clip test's sign check, since behind the eye this projection always puts depth outside ±w, and the lens check, since without one the distance is not a number). In the browser, twelve wheel steps on a loop of **A corridor of loops** five units ahead of the eye leave its target within a loop's width of that loop's depth and the curve drawn within 2 px of the pointer, ahead of the eye, at the target's depth; **+** zooms toward the corridor's far end in the middle of the page, Home clears the depth, and the orthographic keys leave it unset. Links carry the depth, older links have none, and nonfinite ones are refused.

## Named views and the orientation indicator

The 3D drawing and the 4D notebook's 3D image share one turntable: yaw ψ about the vertical y axis and pitch φ toward it, with rows right `r̂ = (cos ψ, 0, sin ψ)`, up `û = (sin φ sin ψ, cos φ, −sin φ cos ψ)` and toward the viewer `b̂ = (−cos φ sin ψ, sin φ, cos φ cos ψ)`. Four named views under each drawing set ψ and φ alone, keeping zoom, pan and the projection:

| View      | ψ    | φ             | Looks along             | Right, up                |
| --------- | ---- | ------------- | ----------------------- | ------------------------ |
| Front     | 0    | 0             | −z                      | x, y                     |
| Side      | π/2  | 0             | +x                      | z, y                     |
| Top       | 0    | 1.5           | −y, 4.1° from vertical  | x, −z (foreshortened)    |
| Isometric | −π/4 | asin(1/√3)    | −(1, 1, 1)/√3           | x and z descending, y up |

Pitch stops at ±1.5 rad, so **Top** looks down from 4.1° (π/2 − 1.5) short of overhead: lengths along z are drawn shortened by sin 1.5 ≈ 0.9975, and y leans in by cos 1.5 ≈ 0.071. From the isometric view the three axes make equal angles with the line of sight and are drawn equally long, √(2/3) of their length. A view is a camera, not a mode: orbiting, panning or the keys continue from it, a link carries the resulting yaw and pitch, and none is recorded by name. A turn stops **Rotate view**, so the view holds, and it is unavailable while an animation drives the camera. Released after playback, the view turned is the released one.

The **orientation indicator** before the views draws each coordinate axis as the drawing shows it: x, y and z along (e·r̂, e·û) on the page, in the axis inks of the 4D drawing, nearer axes over farther ones and farther ones fainter. An axis whose drawn length is under a quarter, within about 14° of the line of sight, is drawn end on: a dot when it points toward the viewer, a ring when away. In 3D it follows the camera actually drawn, including an animation's and a ridden lens's (whose rows are right f × u, up u and toward the viewer −f); in 4D it follows the shadow's camera and is absent with the named views while the drawing is a flat coordinate diagram. Neither is part of the drawing, so no export changes.

**Checks.** Unit tests observe each view through the 3D drawing's own camera: its rows are the table's axes to 10⁻¹², Top's line of sight is π/2 − 1.5 from vertical, and the isometric view draws all three axes √(2/3) long; a turn keeps zoom and pan. Browser tests turn the 3D drawing to each view and orbit on with the keys, follow the indicator (its axes, end-on markers and an orbit animation's turn), check that a turn stops the rotation and waits during playback, and open a copied link at the turned camera. In 4D the tesseract's edges, family k along axis k with no 4D rotation, are horizontal, vertical or end on in Front and Side, and the outer cube's three edge families are drawn equally long in Isometric; a coordinate diagram offers no views, and side by side the views turn the shadow alone.

## Seamless loops

Every animation, in each notebook, spends its duration in one of three ways, set under **Repeat**, at one of two paces, set under **Pace**. The *time* τ ∈ [0, 1] is where the clock and the timeline stand; the *progress* p ∈ [0, 1] is how far the motion has gone, the value a frame is drawn at (a reveal's sample, a track's value, the orbit's angle, a path's camera). Steady and once, the defaults, p = τ, so existing animations keep their timing.

**Repeat.** *Once* plays τ from 0 to 1 and stops. *Loop* plays again and again, wrapping τ from 1 to 0, with p = τ. *Back and forth* plays out and back within the duration: `x = 1 − |1 − 2τ|`, so x reaches 1 at τ = ½ exactly and returns to 0 at τ = 1, and frame τ and frame 1 − τ are drawn at the same progress. A loop and back and forth repeat until paused or stopped; neither ever completes.

**Pace.** *Steady* is p = x. *Ease in and out* is the half cosine `p = (1 − cos πx)/2`: monotone, symmetric (p(x) + p(1 − x) = 1), exactly 0 and 1 at the ends, at rest at both (rate 0) and fastest at x = ½ (rate π/2). Eased back and forth is `(1 − cos 2πτ)/2`, one smooth cosine with no corner at either turn.

**What a loop needs.** A loop joins the motion's end to its start, so it is seamless only when the last frame *is* the first: the same drawing, the same probe and the same camera. Before a loop plays or exports, the notebook draws progress 0 and 1 and compares them, not the entered values: only the drawing knows whether `a` in `cos(t + a)` is periodic, or whether a count changes the pieces. The drawing is compared sample for sample, batch by batch, as the scene draws it with the shown layers: the same kinds, inks, sizes and connections, and every vertex's position within 10⁻⁶ of the study's radius (plus 10⁻⁶ of the coordinate, for single-precision drawing data), its normal and its phase within 10⁻⁶. The camera may differ by whole turns of yaw; its pitch, zoom (in ln z), pan, center and radius must agree to 10⁻⁶. A failure is named: different pieces, a largest gap as a fraction of the radius, different shading, a probe that does not return, or a camera that does not.

So an orbit always loops (its yaw ends 2π on). Parameter tracks loop when each ends one period of the drawing after it starts. A camera path loops when its last view is its first, with any whole turns; **+ Return to view 1** appends a copy of the first view. The probe loops around a closed curve, whose last sample is its first. Drawing along the curve, rising through the box, tracing light and peeling with the cut start one way and end another, so they offer back and forth and never a loop.

Comparing sample for sample is deliberately strict. A closed curve run on until its samples are only relabeled draws the same shape, but a sheet's color (its phase) follows its samples, and a canal surface's circles are triangulated from a frame carried from the first sample, so that end is a different drawing; only a whole period returns every sample to its own place. Rounding can also matter: `sin(2π)` is about −2.4 × 10⁻¹⁶, not 0. On a symmetric grid the level surface cos x + cos y + cos z = 0 runs exactly through grid points, so a level −1.6 sin a meshes differently at a = 0 and a = 2π (different triangles, and lines present only at the start), and the loop is refused. Starting elsewhere in the period avoids such points, and `cos(2π)` rounds to exactly 1, so a level −1.6 cos a returns bit for bit.

**In 2D and 4D.** The 2D and 4D notebooks draw every frame as SVG, the markup playback shows and export rasterizes, so a loop is judged on that markup at progress 0 and 1: number for number within 0.01 of the drawing's units (pixels at 1000 × 760, against coordinates written to three decimals), and character for character otherwise, leaving out the title, description and data attributes that record the inputs (an `a` that has run one period records 2π for the same drawing). A different element, command, color or count of numbers is *different pieces*; otherwise the largest numerical difference is reported in pixels. When the markup differs, both frames are rasterized at the export size on white and compared pixel for pixel, and the loop plays if at most 10⁻⁴ of the pixels differ by more than an eighth of full scale in a channel: an engine may return the same picture in another order after a whole turn. **Section garden**'s sections, turned through 2π, come back as the same segments chained from other vertices, 330 pixels apart in the markup and identical on screen. A motion that has not returned moves whole lines, thousands of pixels.

In 2D only parameter tracks can loop; drawing along the curve and tracing light offer back and forth. In 4D a rotation adds whole turns to two angles, so it returns; a slice passage returns when no section cuts the shape at either end. Other motions (a lift's drift or support, a route, a latitude sweep) start and end in different places and offer back and forth. A family's passage reaches half its spread beyond the shape, so every slice passage, **Section garden**'s included, is empty at both ends and loops.

**A loop's camera path.** With **Smooth**, a loop's path passes through the view where it ends and starts again without stopping: its last view is its first, so both ends take the interior slope of the leg before the seam and the leg after it, `2/(1/δₙ₋₂ + 1/δ₀)` (or 0 at a turning point), and the seam is one more interior view. Steady paths and paths of two views are unchanged.

**Playback and export.** Live playback asks for the time `frac(τ₀ + elapsed/duration)` and orders replies by the unwrapped time, so a reply from before a wrap that arrives after one is dropped. An export of a repeating animation keeps the frame count n = ⌈duration · fps⌉ and the frame durations of a single pass, but draws frame i at τ = i/n, i = 0, …, n − 1: the next frame would be τ = 1, which is τ = 0 again, so no frame appears twice at the seam. Once keeps both endpoints, τ = i/(n − 1). A repeating animated WebP loops forever; MP4 has no loop setting. At 60 fps (offered for WebP as well as MP4) frame delays are 16 or 17 ms, above the 10 ms or less that browsers replace with a longer delay; a WebP stores every frame whole, so it is about twice the size of the same export at 30 fps.

**Checks.** Unit tests check the timing's identities against independent closed forms: steady once the identity, exact endpoints for every repeat and pace, back and forth equal to its outward pass at twice the speed and symmetric about ½, the ease's rates at its ends and middle, and the repeating export's i/n times with the single pass's durations summing to the duration. Simulated playback wraps three times in 3.5 durations, never asks for τ = 1, drops a reply overtaken across a wrap and stops on cancel. Smooth paths in a loop have equal one-sided rates at the seam (within 10⁻³) where an open path's differ (by more than 0.5), still pass through every view and never overshoot. Through the real engine: a phase-shifted trefoil loops for a from 0 to 2π and is refused for 0 to π (a gap) and with a count track (different pieces); a relabeling ninth of a turn is refused and a whole turn plays; an open camera path is refused until **Return to view 1** closes it, and then its ends agree to 10⁻⁹ with a whole turn between them; the probe loops around a closed curve and is refused along an open arc. A full-turn orbit exported once at 15 fps repeats its first frame at the seam (a mean difference of 1.6 gray levels against 16.7 between neighboring frames); looping, the seam is an ordinary step (16.2 against 16.0), decoded by ffmpeg. A back-and-forth export's frame i matches frame n − i, and a repeating WebP, decoded by Chromium, has n frames, a last frame unlike its first, and loop count 0. In 2D and 4D, unit tests check the markup comparison: rounding at the third decimal and metadata are ignored, a moved coordinate is measured, and a gap in a path, an extra point or element, or another color are different pieces. Through the real engine: in 2D an evolute whose third harmonic is phase-shifted by a loops for a from 0 to 2π and wraps its time without completing; a quarter period of a breathing ellipse is refused with its gap in pixels, and half a period of the phase and a count track with different pieces; back and forth and easing give the same progress as in 3D. In 4D every preset's whole turns pass the check and so does every slice passage, **Section garden**'s included; a rotation loop wraps its time; a repeating WebP, decoded by Chromium, has n frames, loop count 0 and a seam that is an ordinary step, and a slice passage out and back has frame i matching frame n − i, empty at both ends. A 60 fps WebP stores, and Chromium decodes, twelve delays of 16 or 17 ms summing to 0.2 s.

## Reference studies

Each of these recipes is a preset, framed independently with equal axis scale. They are mathematical descriptions, not pixel specifications.

- Hypotrochoid: R = 5, r = 2, d = 3, t ∈ [0, 4π], with the base and rolling circles (**Hypotrochoid & its evolute**).
- Offset stack: the polar curve `r(t) = 1 + 0.18 cos(5t)`, t ∈ [0, 2π], with signed offsets d = 0.105k for k = −9, …, 8 (**Flower & its offset stack**).
- Pedal: the ellipse `(2 cos t, 1.1 sin t)` about the pole (1.65, 0.3), t ∈ [0, 2π], with 64 projection constructions (**Ellipse & its pedal**).
- Chords: circle angles t and 4t joined at 200 evenly spaced phases; the chords are drawn, and their envelope is solved separately (**Chords of four**).
- Pursuit: seven equal-speed pursuers starting on the unit regular heptagon. The exact paths are `exp(−tan(π/7)θ)(cos(θ + 2πj/7), sin(θ + 2πj/7))`, where θ is an angle, not time; the connecting polygons are drawn (**Seven pursuers & an evolute**).
- Involute family: the helix `(2 cos t, 2 sin t, t)`, t ∈ [−π, π], anchored at t₀ = 0 with nine string lengths c = −4, −3, …, 4. Each member is a lifted circle involute in the plane `z = c/√5` (**Unwinding a staircase**).
- Spatial pursuit: four unit-speed pursuers from the regular tetrahedron `(±1, 0, 1/√2)`, `(0, ±1, −1/√2)`, ε = 0.005, t ∈ [0, 1.75]. Each path is `(ρ cos θ, ρ sin θ, ρ²/√2)` with `θ = θ₀ + ln(1/ρ)`, captured near t = 1.62 (**Four pursuers on a tetrahedron**).
- Surface focal set: the torus R = 2, r = 0.8 on its outer band `v ∈ [−π/3, π/3]`, with inward normals of reach 2.8. Branch 1 is the axis `z = −2 tan v` and branch 2 the core circle of radius 2; both collapse to curves (**A torus revealing its centers**).
- Caustic: the sphere of radius 1.5 on its inside, below 70° from its lowest point, under light falling straight down. Branch 1 is the surface of revolution of the nephroid, with its cusp at the paraxial focus 0.75 below the center, and branch 2 the axis from there down to `1.5/(2 cos 70°) ≈ 2.19` below the center. The steepest rays reach the lower part of the axis only through the mirror itself, since nothing is occluded (**A spherical bowl's cusped caustic**).
- Refracted caustic ring: the sphere of radius 1.5 on its cap within 55° of its pole, refracting light falling straight down from air into glass (n = 1.5). Branch 2 is the axis, down to the paraxial focus 4.5 below the pole; the receiver on `z = −1.9` crosses branch 1's cusped sheet in a ring brighter than the light inside it, and collects all but under 0.1% of the 4.82 units of beam the cap intercepts (**A glass dome's ring of light**).
- Self-intersecting level surface: the usual immersion of the Klein bottle, `(x² + y² + z² + 2x − 1)[(x² + y² + z² − 2x − 1)² − 8z²] + 16yz(x² + y² + z² − 2x − 1) = 0` (Stewart 1991, as given by MathWorld, with x and y exchanged), in the box `[−2.7, 3.3] × [−3.1, 3.1] × [−3.9, 3.9]`, which contains the whole surface, on 48 cells with one refinement level, seen through at α = 0.3 with dashed hidden lines (**A Klein bottle passing through itself**).
- Camera path: Viviani's curve `(a(1 + cos t), a sin t, 2a sin(t/2))`, a = 1.5, t ∈ [−2π, 2π], where the sphere `x² + y² + z² = 4a²` meets the cylinder `(x − a)² + y² = a²` that touches it inside at (2a, 0, 0); there the curve crosses itself at right angles. Along z it projects to the cylinder's circle; along x to the lemniscate of Gerono `y² = z²(1 − (z/2a)²)`; along y to the parabola `x = 2a − z²/2a`, traced there and back. The camera's turntable axis is y, so the first two are seen exactly at pitch 0 and the third only from 4° short of overhead. The crossing lies on the plane through the study's center facing a camera at yaw 0, so panned to the middle there, the camera turns about it (**Viviani's curve, from every side**).
- Camera path while a parameter moves: the helix `(R cos t, ht, R sin t)`, R = 1.6, h = 0.5, t ∈ [−π, π], with speed v = √(R² + h²) = √2.81, unwound from t₀ = 0 while the string length c runs from −πv to πv. The tangent's y component is the constant h/v and the string runs out at the cusp s = c, so the involute `I_c = r + (c − s)T` has `y = hc/v` at every point: it is level, the involute of the circle below it, and its plane climbs the helix from end to end. Every level view sees it edge-on as one line, which the flight's first leg shows from turning sides; from above (4° short of the axis) it is a circle's involute (**A helix's string, always level**).
- Camera path while light is traced: half a circular cylinder of radius 1.5 about z, `z ∈ [−1.2, 1.2]`, on its inside, under light falling straight down the y axis. Each cross-section is a semicircular mirror under parallel light, so caustic branch 1 is the nephroid `(R/4)(3 sin θ − sin 3θ, −(3 cos θ − cos 3θ))` extruded along z, with its cusp line 0.75 below the axis; branch 2, along the straight rulings, is at infinity. End-on (yaw 0, pitch 0) every section projects onto one, so the sheet is seen as the nephroid. Traced, the light reaches the caustic near the rims first and the cusp last; the flight holds end-on while it closes and ends looking down onto the cusp line (**Following light into a trough**).
- Riding a ray: the paraboloid `(u, v, (u² + v²)/4)` (k₁ = k₂ = 0.5), `u, v ∈ [−1.6, 1.6]` as in the tilted beam but on a 96 × 96 grid, under light 20° off its axis (azimuth 0°, elevation −70°), with rays drawn 2 long. The ray at u = 0.8, v = 0 lies in the plane of the tilt; it reflects at (0.8, 0, 0.16) and passes its two real caustic points, where it touches each caustic sheet, about 1.08 and 1.25 along. The camera follows 0.1 framing radii (about 0.44) behind it, turns over a window of 0.2 radii at the mirror, flies into the two sheets around their cusp, and rests about 1.56 along, just past both points, when the head reaches the ray's drawn end (**Riding a ray through coma**).
- Seamless loop while a parameter moves: the trefoil run on by a, `c(t + a)` with `c(s) = ((2 + cos 3s) cos 2s, (2 + cos 3s) sin 2s, sin 3s)`, t ∈ [0, 2π] on 720 samples, carrying spheres of radius `0.26(1 + 0.85 sin 9t)` fixed in t. Since `|R′| ≤ 0.26 · 0.85 · 9 ≈ 1.99` is below the knot's least speed √13, the envelope exists everywhere. As a runs from 0 to 2π, the nine swellings travel once around the knot and every sphere returns to its own sample, while a smooth closed path circles once through three views (**Beads running around a trefoil**).
- Seamless loop of a level surface: `cos x + cos y + cos z = −1.6 cos a` in `[−π, π]³` on 40³ cells, a from 0 to 2π: caps about the box's corners at a = 0, Schwarz's P surface (level 0) at a = π/2, one drop about the center at a = π, its pieces pinching apart as the level passes ±1. `cos(2π)` rounds to exactly 1, so the last frame is the first bit for bit (**A Schwarz surface breathing**).
- Seamless loop in 2D: the catacaustic of the ellipse (1.4 cos t, sin t), 1,600 samples and 72 rays, lit from a lamp at polar radius 0.55 whose angle θ runs from 0 to 2π over 14 s. The caustic swings and reshapes its cusps as the lamp circles; at θ = 2π the lamp is where it began, so every ray and caustic point returns (**A lamp circling a mirror**).
- Seamless loop in 4D: three latitudes of Hopf fibers, twelve each, on the unit 3-sphere, turned in xw and yz together. Each fiber passes through the projection's pole and opens into an arc through the clipping window, then closes; a half turn already maps the set of fibers onto itself, and a whole turn returns every fiber (**Hopf circles, flowing forever**).
- Back and forth: the gyroid of **A gyroid, cut open** on 48³ cells, without its section, peeled by the cut with normal (1, 1, 1) out and back over 10 s at the eased pace (**A gyroid peeled and regrown**).
- Level surface: the tanglecube `x⁴ − 5x² + y⁴ − 5y² + z⁴ − 5z² = −11.8` in the box `[−2.6, 2.6]³` on 48³ cells. Each term has minimum −6.25 at `±√2.5`, so the eight drops around the minima join along the cube's twelve edges once c passes −12.5: one closed piece of genus 12 − 8 + 1 = 5, Euler characteristic −8, cut by five planes across its diagonal (**The tanglecube**).
- Clifford density: (a, b, c, d) = (−1.4, 1.6, 1, 0.7) from (0.1, 0.1), discarding 1,000 iterates and accumulating the next 800,000, shaded logarithmically. It illustrates, and does not prove, chaotic dynamics (**Clifford attractor**).
